McDOUGALUS Patetit SELF-RAISING FLOUR INPEGIENBs.

Councillor WILKINSON : From my broad view. (Laughter.) When we start this work the whole of Church-street will have to be paved. We can borrow money for new work, but not for the repair of old streets.

Alderman EVANS in reply, said he had carefully worded the motion, and his intention was to set the machinery going. He had used the word" requested " in the motion. Everything must have a move, and if the Paving and Sewerage Committee considered the matter it was as much as he could expect. He could not accept Councillor Wilkinson's figures, and he would let the motion go to the vote, because he intended it to to be a suggestion to the committee, and not a binding resolution.

Councillor LORD :I submit it is binding. Councillor PEMBERTON : If the resolution is passed does it not mean that the work is to be proceeded with?

The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK: In my opinion it would bear that construction. Alderman .EvANs : Under thA circumstances I cannot do anything but withdraw the motion.

MARCH SKIN TROUBLES:

TIME-CRIBBING.

_ At a meeting of the Watch Committee a further letter from the Home Office was read as to the question of "Time-cribbing 7 in cotton mills and asking for re-consideration of this committee's decision. It was resolved, That inasmuch as there is only one cotton mill in this borough, and as the distance of the work- rooms from the public thoroughfares is considerable, this committee cannot see its way to adopt the sugges- tion that the police should follow the exampte adopted in Oldham of dealing with the question." The NtAvoß moved, and Councillor BURTON seconded, the passing of the minutes. Councillor STorr alluded to this minute, and said there was only one cotton mill in Warrington, but it would be as great a crime to crib in one as in twelve. The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK said this WAS a matter for . the internal management of the police, with which the Council had nothing to do. Alderman TINNION : You cannot control all the , actions of the Watch Committee. It has certain powers independently of the Council. The MAYOR: That is so. There are factory inspectors and it is not fair that the police should be put on. Councillor STOTT said the police had jurisdiction in " Time-cribbing " in the matter of the publicans, and it was as great a crime to "time crib" in factories as for a publican to crib five or six minutes after the statute hours. He regretted they could not use the poli6e. Councillor PEMBEnTON : Would it not be competent fora member of the Council to put down a resolution asking the Watch Committee to do this? The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK : Quite so. Councillor PEMBERTON : If they refused to carry out the wishes of the Council we should know what to do when they were about•to be appointed. (Laughter.) The minutes were then agreed to. COUNCILLOR BALL AND THE BOROUGH AUDIT. Councillor BALL, writing to the Finance and Estate Committee, which owing to the hour of meeting he was unable to attend, said : I wanted to suggest to the Committee the advisability of considering a better method of audit of the Cor- poration accounts, and of taking steps for the cost to be provided in the next estimates. I have for some- time felt that the extent and complexity of the Cor- poration accounts have increased go largely that a com- plete cheek by a qualified chartered accountant or firm of accounts is advisable.

I do not think it reasonable to expect that the arith- metical accuracy of the Corporation accounts shall be certified by elective auditors, and the proportion of fee to be obtained under present conditions of audit is not sufficient to pay a fully qualified man, it he is to do the work in anything like the same detail as he would check a private firm's accounts of equal magnitude. I intend no reflection on elective auditors past or present or cm the various book-keepers of the Corpora- tion, but I feel that our system of audit is out of date and inadequate to the magnitude of our affairs. I suggest that the Town Clerk be asked to obtain, say from 30 to 50 towns in England, information on the practice obtaining in those towns and then the Finance Committee consider our position in the light of that information and our needs. Elective auditors would of course still be elected, but their duties would he such as I think were intended in the Act, a reasonable safe- guard against maladministration and their pay very small. lam sorry to write you at such length, but I think the matter important and shall be pleased if you will place my views before the committee. It was resolved, "That the Town Clerk obtain information as to the practice in other towns." The minutes wGre confirmed, on the motion of Alderman FORST-lA.IV, seconded by Councillor WILKINSON.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STANDARD'RATE AGAIN.

Some discussion arose on a minute of the Electricity and Tramways Committee accepting a tender for the supply of _cables and feeder pillar at £293 10s. 2d.

Uotincillor SToTT moved that the recommendation be referred back as he believed the firm in question recently decided to reduce the wages of engineers to 2s. or 3s. ' below the standard rate and consequently union men were withdrawn. They had also employed joiners at 253. a week, whereas for the same number of hours they ought to be paid a little over 22. Aloreover the firm had made use of the Labour Bureau to obtain men at these rates.

COUReiIIOT PEMBERTON asked if, according to the Standing Orders, the tender could be accepted? The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK said he knew nothing of Councillor Stott's information. The Council could accept the tender and if they afterwaids had reason to think that the conditions were as Councillor Stott alleged it would be a matter for inquiry. Councillor Evx:s;s : It is not much use moving an amendment if it can be understood that an inquiry will take place. Councillor STOTT : If the Committee will assure me that they will satisfy themselves that the firm is paying the recognized rate, that will satisfy me. Councillor WILKIXSON promised to make inquiries into the matter, and he thought the firm in question would fare badly in regard to future contracts, if they did not pay the standard rate, The MAYOR said the two lowest tenders were from Germany, woull he, Stott, prefer that the contract should go there? Councillor STroT thought the standing orders should be adhered to.

Counctllor HAWTHORN said that if it were not under- stood that inquiries would be made, he would second the amendment. The MAYOR undertook that the firm should be written to for an explanation. BRIDGE-STREET AND THE TRAMS.

Councillor EVANS called attention to the fact that Bridge-street was being paved, and this presented an opportunity for the continuation of the tramways so that tho cars could start from Market-gate, when the widening was completed. He was informed that if the present opportunity were allowed to pass and it was decided after a short period to put down the lines, the extra cost of the work would be something like 2500.

Councillor WM:ON:SON asked if it would not be possible to lay the street with the understanding that they were going on with the tram lines. He knew at present they had not the power to lay the lines. The MAYorc said that was so. They would have to get special powers to do the work. They only had the power to go as far as Itylands-street, but got permission to go a little up Bridge-street for the purpose of a siding for the trams. The Town Clerk was at present in London, and he (the Mayor) and Mr. Whittle were going to the Board of Trade to see if they would allow them to carry the tram lines 70 yards further. If the Board refused, then they had done the next best thing by ask- ing the Paving and Sewerage Committee to put in a foundation of concrete, so that all that would have to be done when the time came would he to lay the rails. Alderman TtN'N[ox said the Paving and Sewerage Committee had taken the matter in Ond so that there would be no waste of money.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
MONEY FOUND TN CARS. THE LIABILITY OF THE CORPORATION.

Co-tin-c-illo-r—g'iF—.l. alluded to a resolution of the Electricity and Tramways Committee: "That in all cases in which money is found on the cars and is unclaimed for six months, the same shall be paid to the Ifinder, to he used for such purposes as the general body of workmen shall decide." He should like to ask the Deputy Town Clerk whether the liability of the Cor- poration had been considered in the event of a con- siderable suet of money being found and handed to the finder and the owner eventually turning up. Both the Town Clerk and himself were interested in a case that took place in the town two years ago, when it was held that where money was found in a public place,and the owner afterwards turned up for it, even when the money was found by a servant who had taken possession that the owner or proprietor of the public place was liable to the loser of the money. Would it he safe for the resolution to he passed in its present form ? He proposed that the minute be referred back. The DEerrY Tows CF.ERK : I have not considered the resolution, and I don't know the case Councillor Steel refers to. I think in the circlimstances the Council would do wrong to ptss the resolution to-night. The MAYOR : Certain things have been found. The DEeurY TOWN CLERK. : I, may say at once that don't agree with the resolution. I think the com- mittee are tal/4ing too much upon themselves. The resolution was then withdrawn by the Mayor, and the other minutes were agreed to. ' "MR. WILI.I.A.VI BOLTON'S" COSTS. The General Purposes Committee resolved :—That the bill of costs of the defendant Mr. William Bolton in the action of Edelsten v. the Corporation .aud Bolton as taxed at LB) 17s. 11d, be paid. This was agreed to. THE COLLEGE FIELD.

_ The Town Clerk submitted to a recent meeting of the same committee a letter from Messrs. Henry Greenall and Company. stating that the trustees of the Clergy Widows' and Orphans Fund were prepared to accept the sum of £3,000 for the purchase of the plot of land in Manchester-road subject to that sum being clear, all costs being paid by the Corporation, and sub- ject to the covenants contained in the conveyance and lease to the Training College trustees respoctively,dated the sth of September. 1906. _

On the motion Of the .111.tvon, seconded by Alderman TINNION, the minutes were approved.

Why Zam-Buk is Specially Needed in Late Winter and Early Spring.

March is a very critical month for the skin. It fights against big odds all through the winter, and the odds are bigger just now because of the trying weather and because the first crop of Spring pimples is nasty erup- tions may be expected—unless the skin s protected and corrected by Zam-Buk. The Examine, Saturday, March 9, 1907

PARASITIC FISH

Animal parasites usually belong to the lower orders of the animal kingdom and are seldom of higher grade than the worms. Yet there are some notable parasites in the cliss "Pisces." Tho hag-fish, for example, lives inside the cod, or other fish. But it only becomes a parasite in adult life. ' Provided with a suctorial mouth, it attaches itself to the side of the cod, and by means of its single tooth bores its way into the body cavity. The parasitic fish described by Mr. E. Linton in - the American Naturalist, has to find its host in a similar way. This species, con. trary to the general rule in parasitism, preys on an animal of lower grade than itself. Its host, in fact, is one of those curious creatures known as sea-cucumbers, belonging to the zoological order Holothuroidea, —to which group the Beches-de-mer, so highly esteemed by the Chinese as an article of diet, also belong. When this little fish (fierasfer affinis) comes into con- tact with a sea-cucumber, it feels its way along the body till it reaches the vent. It then curls round its tail, and inserts the whiplike end into the aperture. Straightening itself, it then gradu- ally works its way first into the body of the sea- cucumber, by the assistance of the spines on the dorsal and ventral fins. It is said to be able to accomplish an entry in half 'a minute.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH-DEALING FLOWERS

It is well known to modern botanists that cer- tain plants are carnivorous. The common sun- dew (Drosera) of our moors and mosses spreads its little round or spoon-shaped leaves, studded with sensitive and glutinous tentacles, for nc other purpose than to attract and imprison small flies, from which it extracts and assimilates the juices, deriving nourishment' therefrom. "This wonderful little plant," sAys Mr. Scott Elliot in his recently-published 'Romance of Plant. Life,' " shews quite distinctly that there must be some way of sending messages in its leaves. Some- how the message travels from the tentacle which the fly has touched. down the stalk into the leaf, and up into the other tentacles, and tells them that there is sOmething worth stooping for." Central American botanists are accumulating evidence in support of their belief that plants are not only sensitive, which requires little de- monstration, but that they reflect and exercise will power. At all events, says Sir Herbert Maxwell, the Drosera, like epicures of higher organisation. are sadly wanting in discretion. Mr. Scott Elliot tells us that "when they are taken to a greenhouse and experiments are made on them, they run into very great danger. They are almost certain to die of over-feeding or indi- gestion. It is impossible to keep people from giving them too much to eat." The butterwort (Pinquicula) is a lovely little native plant with violet flowers and waxy, sue-

SCIENCE NOTES XND NEWS.

CALCIUM CARBIDE

According. to the Mining World calcium car- bide in the form Of sticks or blocks is now being made in the electric furnace at Niagara Falls, New York. In this form the calcium carbide can be exposed to moist air and handled with- out suffering appreciable decomposition: it can be caused Co give off gas with great regularity when used in a suitable generator, and when the generation of gas is to be stopped this can be done practically instantaneously.

MYSTERIES OF SLEEP.

A curious "break and make" action among the brain cells is thought by Sir William Gowers to explain sleep. Brain activity is attributed to nerve cells, and nerve cords from these divide and subdivide until they end in little knobs. The nerve cells were formerly supposed to be per- manently connected through their terminals. It is now known that these may be separated. and it is concluded that separation occurs during sleep. It is claimed that the theory is supportec by the effect of narcotics.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
CURIOUS METHOD OF MINING

The increasing depth to which it is necessary to gszi in following the vein of kaolin, or potter's clay, mined near Housatonic River in Connecti- cut, caused the introduction of a' novel method of mining. Wells from 50ft. to nearly 200 ft. in depth are driven, and two pipes, the outer 4in. and the inner 2in. in diameter, are introduced. Water at a pressure of 401 b. per square inch is forced through the larger one, bringing with it about 5 per cent. of solid matter, of .which 75 per cent. is pure kaolin.

TO GET BAS-RELIEFS IN' PHOTOGRAPHS. , To obtain a bas-relief effect in photographs, Professor G. Michaud first makes a glass positive from the glass negative, then uses ,these glass plates to make a positive and a negative film. Developed in the same baths the two films con- tract equally on drying. On sensitised paper one film is laid over the other so that the pictures coincide, then one film is slightly moved diagon- ally, causing intense lights and deep shadows to be cast •on opposite sides of reliefs and hollows. Printing is done without a ground glass, in direct sunlight.

MINERS AND ALCOHOL.

Dr. Cesare Biondi calls attention to the fact that miners who work in mining ores containing lead, antimony, or mercury, arc intoxicated by an amount of alcohol which would have no such effect on persons in a normal condition. These persons are in a condition of chronic metallic poisoning, and the alcohol has an unusual effect on the organs. These metals produce an altera- tion in the fine tissues of the nervous elements of some portions of the brain. Alcohol produces similar alterations in the foetuses of animals intc whom alcohol has been injected. Its effect on the already poisoned nerve-coils of the miner i! much more marked than under ordinary circum- stances. The metallic poisoning renders him unable to resist the effects of alcohol, and such persons should not be judged too harshly when they become intoxicated.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SIZE OF PYGMIES.

Mr. S. P. Venner calls attention in the Popu- lar Seirnee Monthly to certain popular errors concerning the race of undersized , men and women known as pygmies. Usually travellers have measured the • most diminutive specimens, and thus an exaggerated idea of their smallness has been produced. The average stature of the pygmies that have- been measured is about 4ft. Bin., but the -best authorities in anthropology say that the real average limit of stature is sft. Some specimens arc taller than that. The pyg,- mks have been found in eight or ten different places in Central Africa. but wherever found they possess certain striking resemblances. The plant furnishing the covering for their huts is the same in the widely separated regions visited by Stanley and Wissmann, and the shape of the huts, a rough hemisphere, is the same. All the pygmies are alike characterised by neglect of agriculture,- by the use of poisoned arrows, and, by the absence of any centralised tribal organisa- tion.

London's Lord Mayor declared the other day that he did not know much about (looking, " But," said he," I know wben a thing's nice." AMBULANCE COMPETITION.

At the Viadnct Institute last week, the ambulance classes of the London and North-Western Railway Com- pany held their competition, for which nine teams entered. They were—Preston. Warrington,. Leyland, Spring Branch, Wigan, Clock Face, Colborne, Peaseley Cross and Earlestown, when some very clever work' was shown. The premier honours were won by Preston, who also won the individual prize. Warrington was second, and Colborne third. A series of exhibitions are given in different parts of Lancashire, and the winner of each event enters for the final, held at Belle Vue in June. The prize—a shield—is given by the London and North-Western Railway Company.

60 YEARS A WESLEYAN MINISTER.

The R.V. Giffard Dorey, who is one of the oldest Wesleyan ministers in England, recently retired and has gone to live at Scarborough. He is 81 years of age, but is halo and hearty. Out of his GO years ministerial life, he worked 17 years in Canada.

THE NEW THEOLOGY. Rev. John Yonge on the Immanence of God. Preaching at Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening the Rev. John Yonge took for his subject "The Scriptural doctrine of the Immanence of God." His discourse was based upon the' words: " Do not I fill heaven and earth, saith the Lord ?" (Jeremiah xxiii. part 24.) He said the doctrine of the Immanence of God declared with absolute and Scriptural truth that God was always everywhere ; that place, and spot and creature could never be formed where He was not filling them with Himself. It was this sublime truth which the New Theology claimed as its fundamental axiom, of " God immanent, indwelling in His universe, the Life of its life, the mind and energy of which all the visible world is only the passing (but continual) manifestation." What, however, it might be specially due to the pro- pounders of the New Theology to acknowledge was that they were recalling attention to the truth of the immanence of God in men; God in humanity; God in human nature; God in the human soul. It was abso- lutely true to say that deep in the inmost inwardness of every human being there was at least a ray of the Divine. What was the moral conscience, which univer- sally existed, and had ever existed in humanity, the inward witness of right and wrong, the continued judge of human thought, imagination.and conduct? What was that but the witness of God in the human soul? God was eternally indwelling in His Creation. God was indwell- ing even in fallen and depraved humanity, seeking and striving to accomplish its salvation. It was of this Divine Omnipotence in the human soul, of this universal indwelling, this immanence of God in men, that the New Theology might at least help to remind and reassure them. Could it but make them all think of the Divine Worker ever at work within them for their redemption and re-creation, bow blessed it would be ! He did not desire to deal in a controversial spirit or even manner, with the subject they had in hand. But it was necessary, he thought, briefly to indicate wherein the doctrine of the immanence of God, as now advo- cAted by some at least of the teachers of the New Theology, might lead their minds astray. If the language that was being employed in setting forth what was meant, was to be accepted and understood literally, then an idea of the immanence, the indwelling of God in men was being conveyed which was full of error and of evil. For example, one of its teachers had said : "Human personality is not a thing which can exist apart from God." So far so good. But this teacher of the New Theology went on to say to his reader: , " You are yourself God: You never have been anything else !" Again, he said : " God and man are not two separate existences : they are one." And yet further be said: "Between Deity and man is no dividing line!" What had they here? Did this appear to them, as in , any sense or interpretation, what they found in Scrip- ! lure? Where i was the text n Old Testament or in New that could authenticate any snch affirmation as this ? No dividing line? What at•out the Apostolic word which spoke in corroboration of the older pro-

phetic declaration, concerning men: "They are all under sin." As it is written, there is none righteous,no not one ! There is none that doeth good, no, not one . . . . . All the world becomes guilty before God." But according to the New Theology "I am myself God : I have never been anything else. Between Deity and me there is no dividing line." And he (the preacher) recalled the scriptural pourtrayals of a Dread Final Day, when the Divine Judge Eternal would receive into the rewarding life of heaven those that had their neaps written in the Lamb's Book of Life, and would banish from His presence all such as had persiated in evil aild wrong. This doctrine of the immanence of God in man was not Scriptural, end was unacceptable by those who accepted the doctrines of the Bible. Any theory of God which covered over, or obscured, or blurred the line of moral difference which stood pro- foundly and deeply marked between us and the infinitely Perfect and Holy God must be erroneous and deceptive. The doctrine of God's immanence was being mis- used so as to obscure the - equal doctrine and truth- of God's transcendence. We might not, in recognizing more clearly the wondrous fact of the Divine indwelling in the universe of nature and the universe of man, lose sight of the equally great fact of His transcendence over all. He was over all and above all. We must not permit any teaching to make no oblivious of God's infinite and peerless supremacy.

Boys' Fatal Collision Mr. Brighouse held an inquest at Warrington on Monday concerning the death of Ernest Bolton, the four-year-old son of James and Lucy Bolton,7,oldham- street. On Thursday evening of last week the boy was playing with several others round a lamp post opposite his home. His mother was standing at the door talking to a neighbour when she suddenly noticed him lying on the footpath unconscious. She was of the opinion that her son collided with one of his corn- panionS and fell on the back of his head. Dr. Lang,- dale informed the police that the boy died from con- cussion of the brain. THE NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL AT LEEDS.

[BY CIRALE3 E. PARTZER.I "Whereunto will this thing grow ? "

The gathering at Leeds is the largest ever yet held. There are 1,400 delegates including several from abroad, and 300 personal members. The vast Coliseum is crowded at every gathering, - Dr. Rendel Harris of Birmingham is a very popular president. He is a great traveller, an arcimologist, sociel )gist, classical scholar, biblical critic, mystic, humanitarian, Friend, and much more. Indeed it might be said it is scarcely worth being what Dr. Harris is not. Dr. Harris is the director or the Friends, Settlement for social and. religious study at Wood- brook° near Birmingham, the aim of which is in the Doctor's own words"To help young men and women to think out, to study social and religious studies, to prepare them for practical service.". It might be thought that the representatives of the Free Churches would meet to shed tears over the dead Education Bill. There appears no disposition to do this. Dr. Harris himself said in his presidential address: "I absolutely decline to mourn over the decease and interment of the late Bill. There was structural weakness in the poor child which made it scarcely worth insuring "and his fellow Free Church- men end irsed this view. More's the pity say I. But as I view the question more from a citizen's point of view than a Free Churchman's, I feel the loss of Mr. Birrell's Bill was a great national calamity. But out of apparent evil may come good, and it may be the next Bill will be of a more logical character and will he more national in its character.

As to secular education the president's pronounce- ment was significant. He said : "I am satisfied that no solution is at the present moment practical except that of secular education, supplemented if possible by the activities of the Churches and without any offensive negative attitude towards the Bible and religion." These words are rather nebulous, but it was signi- ficant that the statement about secular education received- very hearty approval from a considerable number of the delegates. Sir Oliver Lodge referred to this later in the day and appeared to have taken it as an indication of a secular leaning on the part of the Free Churches. It is well for us who are opposed to this solution to remember that the objection to any form Of religious education being given by State servants and paid for by State funds, springs ,not from an indifference to religion but from a belief that it is so important, so spiritual, so sacred a thing that the State is altogether disqualified. The address of Sir Oliver Lodge was one of the features of the day. I understood that the address is in part the basis of an article which is to appear in the " Hibbert Journal" for April. I should like to see the article in print before expressing a final opinion. We were all struck with the breadth of view, the largeness of vision which characterized it through- out. It is true that Dr. Lodge spoke as a Churchman hut it was with all the breadth of a scientist and all the charity of a Christian. He laid a firm bold on the things that matter most and he put the small things in their proper place. He pleaded for a National Ohurch but it was a Church formed of all who love the Lord Jesus Christ in sincerity and truth. He would sever the State's connection with such a church in so far as it interfered with doctrine, made appointments or administered State funds. It is difficult from bearing the address once exactly to know what concrete connection the National Church which the Doctor had in his mind would have with the State. Every Christian Church was to be a branch of this National Church. The address was intensely interest- ing and revealed a mind entirely free from ecclesiastical incumbrances whether derived from the Established or Free Church environment. It seemed like a breeze from a higher field of thought. Almost every sentence bore evidence of a mind that had travelled far and in its travels enlarged its grasp of essential things. If Sir Oliver Lodge could be induced to attend all large gatherings of the Christian Churches and speak to them as he has sppken to us at Leeds to-day he would do a work of enlarging the vision and broaden- ing the sympathies of Christian people the value of which it would be difficult to over estimate.

WEDNESDAY : —Anyone, who attends all the meetings in connection with this gathering has an appetite which I cannot lay any claim to. As a result of this de6ciency I have only attended some of the gatherings to-day. I was fortunate,lowever,in being present when the subject of the Congo Atrocities cime up for con- sideration. _ _ .

The President called upon the Rev. J. H. Harris to introduce the question. Who this fresh coloured young man was few of us knew. But very soon we were with him in the mission field, his own mission field. He soon gripped the vast audience. There was no rhetoric, no trained eloquence, no tricks of oratory or any of the other stock in trade of speakers who sway large audiences. But there was something better. He had a tale to tell warm from his own heart, founded on what he himself knew ; what his o-.vn eyes had seen, and what his own heart had felt—the tale brought tears to many eyes. It was a tale of man's inhumanity to man. A ghastly record of cruel- ties to an inferior race for the establishment of racial ascendancy with a view to financial gain. Man sacri- ficed to matter. Justice and mercy sacrificed to the- lust for gold. I doubt if the cruelties of slavery could. produce anything from its dark pages so black as the. tale told by this Oongo Missionary. It was a tale of hell let loose, cruelty triumphant. Mr. E. D. Morel who has done so much and given years of his life to the removal of this infamy, seconded the resolution. He told us that the tale told by Mr. Harris o a .single- mission could be duplicated by reports from every corner of a land ten times as large as Great Britain. Dr. Meyer seized the opportunity ; he revealed the fact that Mr. Harris and his noble wife were as his own children, who had gone out to the Mission field from his own Church. Ere proposed that the passing of the resolution ought to be accompanied by a promise and a dedication. The audience were asked to promise to work in this cause and dedicate them- selves to this work. The . resolution was passed by a solemn standing vote and the promise given by 3,000 uplifted hands. But il may be asked why there should be any necessity of rousing the national conscience on this matter. It. must be clear to everyone who studies the international negotiations which led up to the Act of the West African Conference which handed over to King Leopold of Belguim the lordship of this. land— anyone who studies the Act itself will be forced to the conclusion that this King has been Ills° to his trust. Where is the " civilizing and humanitarian work " which was to be the outcome of this rule. Where is the carrying out of those principles from which the "general interests of humanity" were to profit ? Not one of the promises has been kept nor one of these hopes has been fulfilled. Surely it is the duty and the right of .the nations who gave the trust into the hands of the King on clearly defined conditions to withdraw that trust when every condition has been violated. England ought to take the lead. It ought not to be necessary to bring pressure on the Government, but if pressure has to be brought it ought not alone to be the work of the" Noncon- formist Conscience." Every citizen ougbt to take his. share in the responsibility and work and this without reference to creed or party. But whether the nation speaks in one voice or 20, let the Free Churches no longer keep silence.

POLICE COURT AND PRISON GATE MISSION. The work of the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission of tho Liverpool Diocesan C.E.T.S. is carried on at every magistrates' court in Liverpool and South- West Lancashire. During 233 visits made in the courts in February 1151 persons were conversed with, of whom 549 were subsequently visited in their abodes. At the Mission Room near Walton Prison, which is epen every week-day from 6.30 to 8.15 a.m.,648 discharged prisoners were provided with breakfast and otherwise helped by counsel, work, &c.; 58 persons were visited in their abodes from the prisons; 27 tickets for work at the Society's Firewood and Mat Factory, Bootle, were given to men from the courts and prisons, and seven other men were also assisted to employment; 45 women, girls and boys were provided with work, taken to homes, and sent back to friends ; 114 persons were discharged into the care of the missionaries, or other- wise assisted at the request of the magistrates ; 67 persons were also helped at the suggestion of the court officials and the police. In tLree cases men retained their employment through the intercession of mission- aries with employers. In 29 instances clothing was given to enable men and women to accept work. In 133 cases food was supplied to some of these where the husband and father was in prison. Ninety-five persons were visited at the request of parents and friends to give advice, &c., where the conduct if persisted in might lead to a charge at the police court and a term of imprisonment. An aggregate of 251 persons called upon the missionaries by arrangements to be helped in various ways. Two hundred and forty-four letters were written in the efforts at prevention and roclamatian. The names and addresses of 27 persons were sent to ministers of religion for oversight. Fifty-two meetings were addressed, and 139 persons signed the pledge of total abstinence, &c., &c.

THE SUGGESTER OF CRIME.

By MORLEY ROBERTS,

Author of "The Western Colonies," "Red Earth," "The Earth Mother," "The Circassian," &c., &c.

Baween Bethune and Buller, who were both members of the Asiatic Club, there was at once a remarkable likeness and a remarkable antipathy. If by any chance a member called Buller "Bethune," when the lights were low, it annoyed him very much; and when Bethune was hailed. as "Buller,-' he damned the man wha did it incontinently, glared and often left the room. And yet, beyond the dislike which often shews itself -among those who accidentally resemble each other, there was no real reason for this common hatred. "q just hate him, that's all," said Buller, when Singleton asked him why. "But why, again?" cried Singleton. _

"Oh, because," replied Butler, iFith the well- known American aposiopesis which is so convincing. Bethune liked very few men in the club, but sometimes he talked with Singleton, who rather prided himself on a power he had of being all things to all men. If in the course of talk he could hint•disparagement of Buller, Bethune always did it. Buller was the only novelist in the club, which was chiefly made up of Anglo-Indians and retired Chinese Customs men. Bethune's speciality was ethnology. and his chief delight the Andaman Islanders. P'He had fads on hypnotism, thought transference, the transmigration of souls, theosophy, voodooism, and occult science generally. "After observing the few idiots I know who'd° it, I've come to the conclusion that only asses and failures write fiction," said Bethune. "Is not ethnology a form of fiction?" asked Singleton. Bethune ignored the feeble joke and jeered at Buller's latest novel.

"What a fool, a weak fool, he is. And yet he writes of violent crimes."

For Buller's special pleasure was the construction and psychology of murder stories. And, as a matter of fact, he did them very well. "But he's not bad at psychology," suggested Singleton. "Only he knows nothing about it," said Bethune, "and he talks of thought transference!" He was drinking pretty heavily just then, and i showed signs of it n the tremor of his hands. "I am telepathetic to my finger tips," he cried, presently, "what folks call intuition is thought transference. Now I can always tell what that fool Buller is thinking of!" He tapped Singleton on the knee, and glared at him with a blood-shot eye. "Ain't it curious?"

"What P" asked Singleton with a little shiver.

"How some men's minds leak! Now he leaks murder. He's always thinking of it; with poison or knives, or a silly piece of occultism." "Of course, he writes it." "I know it. Do you think I read what he writes P" He looked unutterable contempt, and drank steadily in silence. After a quarter of an hour's muttering contemplation he burst out again. "Now you look here, Singleton," he hiccupped, "I've just got hold of the very cleverest notion you ever heard of. Look here,l don't know that I like you so very much, for you're too friendly with that mountainous ass Buller, but you're intelligent,ain't you, and if it weren't for your talking a lot with him, I'd like you. And you are intelligent, ain't you?"

"I hope so," said Singleton staring, "It's my endeavour to be, and as to poor Buller,why he's not so bad when you know him."

"Don't want to," cried Bethune with a devilisl- queer look in his eyes, "but I'm telling you, an why don't you listen, I've the rippingest funn3 notion. Now who murders who ?"

Singleton stared. "Is it a riddle ? " Bethune snorted.

"This is my notion, and I tell you it's horrid. Nc man murders another unless the murdered chap putt the notion into his head. D'ye see ? " Singleton saw, but demurred. "Facts are against you." "Facts," sneered Bethune, "facts, what are facts, but what we think facts, and if we think crookec they're not facts, and how d'ye know we don't think crooked? I thought you were intelligent. Nom you see. A. doesn't like B. and B. doesn't like A., and presently A. happens to think that perhaps B. might kill him. B. gets the notion transferred tc him and does it. Now who is it had killed who i Why, A. has killed himself, because he was a coward." Singleton laughed.

"Then A. ought to be hanged," he cried.

"He's dead," said Bethune, with an ugly grin, "But B. ought to be compensated for the trouble hE was put to. Oh, this will explain a lot of things I haven't time to tell you all of 'em. But it's a ripping notion and I'll write a paper on it." He shut his eyes, perhaps to work it out, and Singleton left him snoring. Bethune was always turned out of the club at half-past one, and it took some courage on the part of the staff to wake him. Naturally they hated tht sight of him, for if they disturbed him before thE exact hour of closing he swore and rebuked them aE if they were pariahs, and if they left it to the last he never got away for a quarter of an hour. For he would have one last brandy. And just now he was drinking harder than usual. "I wish, oh, I wish I had him on the table,' said one old pathologist eagerly; "he'd be verb interesting." Many of the other members were interested in the notion of having him turned out of the club some way or another, for he was rapidly becoming s scandal. Singleton was on the House Committee, and many of the old boys came to him. "Seven and twenty brandies and sodas on a Sunday afternoon seem rather a lot," he owned wher one statistician brought him exact figures. "He's going mad," said others who backed the man with the details.

"Was he ever anything else?" asked Singleton ir despair.

But now he was very bad and jerky and a: tremulous as jelly. His eyes glared sometimes likE the unpigmented eyes of animals. Singleton went to him, and with much vain circumlocution warned him that it was really time he pulled up. And all he said was—

"It won't be my fault." "What won't be your fault ?" asked Singleton,

"It won't be, you tell him so," he repeated with an ugly grin. So Singleton shrugged his shoulders at this insane irrelevance, and took his departure. Finding Buller in his library he spoke about thif madman-.

"I shall really be glad 'when he's dead," cried Buller. "But upon my soul, much as I dislike him, he's really very interesting. Now isn't he? I'm using him in a story, a most gruesome story. I'll tell you the notion." He sketched out his latest yarn and getting excited told it with many details and appropriate gestures. "So you see, it's really the autobiography of a murdered man, who knows he will be murdered and knows who.will do it."

"Can you work it really ? " rwked Singleton doubt- fully.

"You bet," said Buller, "I do believe I've done it very cleverly; and it's most ingenious, and the murder's not based on any true motive but just in an antipathy, a violent diseased antipathy in the murderer. And that's why I'm interested in that mad Bethune, for I've, taken him as my model." "Do you always work with someone in you/ had?"

"Mostly," cried Buller.

"Then who's your murdered man?"

He tapped his shirt front twice.

"Myself, for the sake of the true psychology. Now you chaps who don't write can't understand what pleasure I can get out of my work. I've been through astounding mental experiences on this theory. It's mental travel. exploration. By Jove! I've had to get up and lock my door as I wrote! I was really frightened, and half saw Bethune crouched outside with a hatchet in his hand. It gave me a cold sweat!" He smacked his lips with a curious gusto.

"Rather you than me," said Singleton. "I'm dashed if I think it a healthy thing to do." "Oh rot," cried Buller. "Well, let me know when it's out, and I'll iead it," said Singleton. "But the public want to be amused."

"They want to be interested. And I'll interest 'em," nodded Buller, "I'll interest 'em. Now you ,ee if this story of mine doesn't have a remarkable success."

' "Here's to three columns of criticism on your murder," said Singleton finishing his whiskey, 'aid good-night." • sztlortly after this Sineletnn was deputed to convey 4 hint to Bethune that lie was going far too strong an alcohol.

"You really, really mustn't," he said to the ffreck.

"Mustn't what?" he snarled.

"Mustn't carry on so publicly and get so very nuch mops and brooms," said -Singleton. "Now, twenty brandies and sodas one after the other are rather, rather, ain't they?" Bethune peered at him with a far-away look and evidently paid little attention. It annoyed the land and inoffensive Singleton. "If you don't stop it, there'll be trouble," he cried little less blandly than usual. Bethune nodded absently.

"Yes, if I don't stop it, there'll be trouble. If I lon't stop it there'll be trouble. If I don't stop —" "Oh, stop that," cried Singleton, and Bethune g-rinned. "I can't stop that other idea," he muttered. "What idea?" Bethune woke up and tapped Singleton on thA knee.

"About thought transference, telepathy. You laugh, but I believe. Because I know. Now this is the story of it. A. doesn't like B. (and B. might stand for Bethune), and B. doesn't like A. So by- and-bye A. happens to think B. might like to kill him. Now if he does it, who does it? D'ye see?" He hiccupped and leered oddly and Singleton shivered. Why, the man was as Mad as a March hare!

"You told me that before," he cried with a look of aversion which Bethune didn't notice.

"Did I? Then if I did, isn't it true? And it won't be my fault." He paused and mumbled a little and then his mind apparently got switched off on .another line of thought. "Why don't you get rid of Buller? I hate him. He's got a shining bald spot in the top of his head. And I always know what he's thinking of just under that bald spot." He got up then and left the room. A moment later he thrust his head in again. . "Tell him not to do it, Singleton. Or it won't be my fault." So Singleton went to the committee and reported that Bethune was raving mad. And when they wouldn't or couldn't do anything, he refused to dry- nurse Bethune any more, and growled about it with Buller, whom he found an entertaining companion. For Buller had a picturesque, creepy way of telling yarns and suggesting diabolical situations. "How's your new book getting on ?" asked Singleton. .

"—Slow but sure," cried Buller. "Now, I've a notion one shouldn't work too - quickly if a slow cumulative effect is wanted, or slowly if the story is to run swiftly like a rapid crescendo, ending in a smash of cymbals and a blare of brass."

"And your is slow?" He nodded cheerfully. "Slow cumulative touches of horror•, a rich im- pasto (as the art critics say) of vampire-like devilry. Oh, but it's odd to • weave artistic tapestry and choose the colours and see the pattern grow, sug- gesting other combinations which were quite hidden and- yet obvious when one sees them. I like your sudden grotesque, the known unknown. Crack the nut and the kernel falls out. I'm deep in the middle of the book now, and I'm landscape-garden- ing in my villain's mind, planting him with poisons." "Don't take any," said Singleton. "I shiver apprehensively," cried Buller. He bent down to pick up a match, and Singleton saw his bald spot. "By the way, Buller, I forgot to tell you that that mad Bethune said you weren't to do it.' "Do what?" "I don't know," said Singleton, laughing, "but just that." "He ought to be turned out of the club," said Buller, angrily, "it's preposterous he's allowed here. And to say I'm not to do it? Not to do what, I'd like to know. Look at him, perhaps! "Do you ever think him dangerous P" Buller lifted a peaked eye-brow. "No, no, certainly not. And yet--" "He might be ?" "But if to me, I could smash him easy." Buller was certainly a strongish man, "About my book," he said, presently, glad to talk of his work, "I wonder whether the crazy lunatic will recognise the flattering portrait I'm drawing of him. I've made him a homicide by nature." He pondered a moment. "By Jove, Singleton, I'll get you to read it, if you will. Of course, I don't want to annoy the poor devil, and you will be able to tell me if he'll tumble to it." "I'll read it," said Singleton.

And next day Buller left a bundle of MS. for him. He picked it up, and, marching into the iittle smok ing-room, found Bethune there. As he sat reading he felt that Bethune's eye was on him, and it made him uncoaeortable.

"I wish you wouldn't," be cried at last, and. Bethune grinned curiously and turned away. Per- haps he noticed and knew the handwriting, for he spoke at last. "Did you tell Buller what I said?"

Singleton was deep in strange psychologic devilries. when suddenly the lunatic's crazy face peered at hint He started. "0b.:0h," cried Singleton, irritably, "and what did ycu say?" "I said he wasn't to do it," snapped Bethune; "what's that you're reading?" "Writing," said Singleton, shortly. "Buller 's story P" Singleton stared at him. "Did you recognise the writing?" He sneered and grinned most unplea,singly. "You know better, Singleton. Oh, I tell you it won't be my fault." He was again on this mad notion of something not being his fault. And he began repeating his idiotic formula:

"Now, if A. hates 8.-" Singleton jumped up, grabbed his papers and left the room, being now quite of Buller's opinion that it was time for Bethune to be in an asylum. He found in this story of Buller's a most remark- able analysis of a murderer's mind. He took the view that every murderer must be insane, and sup- ported this thesis with infinite skill in peculiarly small details. But the character in the book who was intended to commit murder, or try to, was not very much like Bethune. _

It took Singleton odd hours during four days to read the whole of the MS. which contained about three-parts of the story. Once by accident he left the papers in the room with Bethune, and was away for an hour. When he came back he looked at Bethune suspiciously. but did not see any reason to think he had read the MS. And yet he certainly might have looked at it. He sat in a dark corner and talked to himself in a most distressing way. Singleton could not stand it, and changed into the reading-room. Some days afterwards they met in the street, and Singleton was shocked at the man's appearance, He had a curious glaze in his eyes. and seemed as if he were staring through a veil. His dress was never much to boast of, though lie had about £BOO a year, which he drew as a retired Indian Civil servant; but now he was unbrushed and unkempt. "Look here, Singleton," he said sulle4ly, "you tell Buller I know all about his tricks, and I won't have any mumbo-jumbo played on me. It's past a joke. and I wouldn't stand it even from a friend." "Why, man alive," said Singleton, "he never speaks to you!" "He does it all the same," cried Bethune angrily, and he writes, writes, writes!"

• He nodded at Singleton. "Oh,. what a fool you are, Singleton," he said suddenly, "why, hang me, you're an Atheist!" And having hurled that absurd charge, he walked away.

That night Bethune made things very pleasant at the club, for they said he saw rats or something peculiar in the card-room, and to keep him quiet, they brought the cat into the room. At first Bethune was very much pleased, and though it was rather an undersized animal, he swore it was the biggest cat he had ever seen. But presently he cried out for it to be taken away.

"It's growing big, on, it's swelling. oh, my God, it's getting bigger." he screamed. "Oh, oh, it's as big as a tiger! Look; at its stripes! "

And before any one could say a word he went off into a fit, and frightened four old chaps almost to death. When he came to, after a most unpleasing time of it, he still asserted there was a tiger about somewhere, and was with difficulty put into a cab and sent home. - - - -

He will talk of little else than thought transference, and is full of strange stories about it. His chief theory is that crime is suggested by precautions against it, and if anyone controverts him he gets excited and very angry. THE ARMY SCHEME.

Captain Crosficid's Criticisms.

THE POSITION OF THE VOLUNTEERS.

Captain G. R. Crosfield addressed the Warrington Junior Liberal Association on Monday evening on "Mr. Haldane's administration at the War Office." Councillor Hats thorn presided over a fair attendance. Captain CROSFIELD said he would take it that the majority of those present, while advocating that as many differences as possible should be settled by arbitra- don, fully recognized that the day had not yet come— in fact was a long way off—when everything could be settled by arbitration alone. In other words he believed the majority thought it was necessary to be prepared for war. With regard to the new scheme Mr. Haldane, took up the army and divided it into two lines. Up to now it had been divided into ! three—the regular army which was entirely ! professional, a semi-professional army called I the militia, and a purely voluntary force including the yeomanry and the volunteers. Mr. Haldane retained the regular army, caused the militia to go partly into the regulars aud partly into the volunteers, and built up the volunteers into brigades and divisions, seeing that they had the requisite amount of artillery and cavalry, and in that way he got his second line. The I daties of the regular army were to guard our empire,' and the bulk of the army abroad was in India. Mr. Haldane said that the regular army should be prepared to mobilise 150,000 men at any given moment, instead of 100,000 under present conditions. By filling up certain gaps in the forces Mr. Haldane increased the number available to the required 150,000, which could be mobilised at any time and sent abroad to reinforce that portion of the regular army at present over-seas. As to the duties of the second line of defence, Mr. Haldane stated clearly that they were as follows :—to man the naval forts, to repel raids, and to reinforce by voluntary service the regular army over seas in case of a great emergency. It was thus evident that Mr. Haldane, whom they were afraid at. one time was going to attach , himself entirely to the" blue water" school had now gone over to the "invasion bogey" school, and he (Captain Crosfield) was delighted that he had done so, because it was a fatuous policy to put all their eggs into one basket and trust to the navy only. At present the Volunteers were about 250,000 in numbers, the militia about So,ooo,and the yeomanry about 26,000. Mr. Haldane was going to do away with the militia and create 72 training battalions which would take the place of the depots. He was going to form by means of those training battalions a special short service reserve where the class of men who now went into the militia would be trained for six months the first year, and go up for repetition courses of fifteen days each for the next six years. In that way he made use of the sub- stance of the militia and brought them, according to his idea, more into touch with the regulary army. He further said that the militia who did not wish to have their substance taken away like that could re-form them- selves on voluntary lines--in fact simply be what were .termed inter-volunteer battalions. He was going to Increase the volunteers in numbers and the volunteers and yeomanry together were to turn out a force about 300,000 strong With regard to

. , THE TRAINING OF VOLUNTEERS Mr. Haldane made a great many alterations. Camps were to be compulsory either for eight or fifteen days; a man must join for four years and if he wished to leave before the expiration of that period be must give three months' notice and pay £5; and in case this'country _was involved in a war and the regular army was abroad, as it was in the South African war, the whole of the auxiliary forces were to be mobilized for six months and, if necessary, they would be asked to volunteer to enforce the regular army over-seas. The administration of the auxiliary forces was to be placed in the hands of the County Associations, presided over by the Lord-Lieutenant of each county, and probably the colonel of each battalion would be placed on the association, and labour and capital and the staff of the district would also be represented. Those associations had nothing to do with I the training or the command of the forces but simply looked after the administration as far as finances and recruiting were concerned. Mr. Haldane hoped that eventually the brigades and even the divisions would be commanded by volunteer officers. He (Captain Cros- field) did not agree with him, because he thought there were very few volunteer officers who were fit to be colonels, let alone generals, and to have a really efficient force he would like to see the colonel of each battalion a regular officer. War was becoming more and more a complicated affair, and the ordinary amateur bad not the time to fit himself for such arduous duties as would devolve upon the heads of the forces in time of war. Proceeding to

CRITICISE THE SCHEME

Captain Crosfield said he must frankly confess that he thought Mr. Haldane had made a wonderfully good job of a very difficult, in fact a very bad business. He had probably done the best possible, considering the limita- tions imposed upon him—limitations of an expandittre of £28,000,000 and of voluntary service. As to whether he would be able to get the numbers which be required for his second line with such stringent conditions. be (Captain Crostleld) very much doubted it, and he was afraid that these stringent conditions 'could not pass through : the fines would be reduced or done away %kith, the compulsory camps would not be strictly adhered to, the compulsory mobilization for six months would he done away with,aud then we would be exactly where we were at present, with the addition that the volunteer battalions had been properly organ- ized—which should have been done years ago—and that the county associations might help matters forward. It was the fact that if his measure went through, Mr. Haldane would enhance the efficiency of the Volunteer forces. How far did that meet the agitation and the demands of the National Service League? In the first place the league thought it was

===== 0002601_19070309_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
A FATAL POLICY

to wait till the danger arose before training, and it was far better and more equitable, and far less of a hardship for a young man to put in three or six months training at a comparatively early age, than to be suddenly called out for six months when he might have become advanced in years and attained a responsible business position. Mr. Haldane would certainly not be able, if be retained his present stringent conditions, to get his men. A great many volunteers would think twice before joining a force where they were absolutely obliged to go to camp, fined if they wanted to leave, and bad the six months mobilization hanging over their heads. The League had always con- tended that the minimum amount of training that would suffice to enable the second line of defence to stand up against thoroughly disciplined troops was three months. According to Mr. Haldane's scheme volunteers who had only had eight or fifteen days' consecutive training, with odd hours at night, might be called upon to repel a raid or invasion without any additional train- ing. As long as they admitted that there was a need for the home defence army, they must be prepared to see that that army was sufficiently trained to be serviceable when it was required. Mr. Haldane's proposals did not in the very slightest weaken the arguments or the-work of the National Service League. They

WISHED MR. HAL DANE ALL SUCCESS.

They thought he bad done the best with the voluntary system and Mr. Haldane himself admitted that this was a lastattempt and if it failed there was nothing for it but to resort to universal train' ng.The League as a body were perfectly prepared to help him in every possible way by stirring up patriotism and inducing men to join-under the new conditions, Lot that did not prevent them in any way from going on with their work of educating the public to see that the only satisfactory and just solution of the question was not to put the burden on the willing horse but to distr.ibute the load equally by each man doing his share. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN considered that on the whole Mr. Haldane had made an honest attempt to do the best he could with the material at his disposal and continuing the voluntary system. If the scheme proved a failure Captain Crosfleld's proposals could be tried, but Mr. Haldane's proposals appeared to be feasible and if taken up in a patriotic spirit by the people of those islands, realizing the necessity there was for a certain amount of defence in case of emergency, he thought they would be able to congratulate themselves that the Liberal Government had brought forward such a scheme. (Applause.) A discussion followed.

Mr. ADAMS believed we should do without soldiers altogether, but if we had to have an army it was as well to have a good one. He believed in voluntary ser- vice and hoped Mr. Haldane's scheme would nat be the stepping stone to compulsory service. Captain CROSFIELD asked Mr. Adams if he would rather be forced to put in the training proposed by the League or see his family suffer indignities and the in vader? Mr. over-run by an an Mr. ADAMS thought it was not necessary for every- one to go into training. If the volunteers were given a better chance, we would be all right. Mr. GALLON thought Mr. Haldane's scheme was fore- doomed to failure. He was sorry Mr. Haldane did not go boldly to the country and say: "People have played with the question long enough. We will go in for compulsory service and make everyone take a proper share." Captain CROSFIELD agreed that it was a pity Mr. Haldane could not take a bolder line, but they did not know the difficulties he probably had to face in the Cabinet. They must give him credit for tackling the question in the best way he could under the circum- stances.

Messrs. A. TILLING and R. LLOYD JONES also took part in the debate, and a vote of thanks was accorded to Captain Croslleld.

" Receivers Worse than Thieves."

At the Pollee Court on Tuesday, before Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes (presiding) and W. Richardson, Thomas Clarke, a greengrocer, Cartwright-street, was charged with stealing or receiving, knowing the same to have been stolen, some time during February, 141 b. of brass punchitigs, value about 95., the property of Messrs. Greening and Sons Ltd., Bewsey-road. Prisozer was represented by Mr. Steel.—Prisoner sold the punchings to Alice Matthews, a metal broker, in. Lythgoes-lane, for about 9s. Prisoner was arrested on Monday night. He then said he had buught them from a man named Parker, employed at Messrs Greening's. Parker was brought to the Police Station, but there was not sufficient evidence to detain him., and he was allowed t,) go. Prisoner was charged, and he replied "I have nothing to say."—Mr. Steel said prisoner pleaded guilty to receiving the punchings, knowing the same to have been stolen, but on the plea that he did not make sufficient inqutries as to where Parker had got' the punchings from, and therefore, in law, he would be liable to receiving them. Prisoner had acted foolishly, and he asked the Bench to deal leniently with him. He had a previous good character.— Charles Saunders, foreman at the works, said Parker was a machinernan at the works, but he was not in the punching department. In answer to Mr. Steel, witness said Parker could easily get the punchings.—The Chairman said the Bench considered it a serious offence, because if there were no receivers there would be no thieves. Receivers, in his opinion, Were worse than thieves. They had considered his previous good character,and he would be fined 40s. and costs (13 1s) with the alternative of a month's imprisonment.

All bystanders at Lodz who did not raise tbeir hats as the hearse containing the body of a murdered captain passed were sabred by the escort, many being badly injured.

Cricket Club Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club was held at the Assembly Rooms, Lion Hotel, on Monday evening unter the presidency of Mr. A. Silcock who was supported by Messrs. J. Richardson D. Tinnion (hon. auditor), and C. H. Unsworth (hon. secretary). The Hon. Secretary, in presenting the accounts, stated that the subscriptions during the season amounted to £207 19s. 6d.; gate receipts, • is. 6d.; rents, £44 55.; House Committee, £29 13s. 10d. The expenditure including rent, rates and taxes, :t5B us. 10d. ; cost of matches, £34 Os. 6d. ; keeping ground, £54 19s. ; repairs to flagstaff, £lO 18s. ; repairs to fences,.£l3 9s. 7d.; mowing machines, • is. 2d. There was a loss on the year's working of little over .£l2. The accounts were unanimously passed. The Retiring President, thanked the members for their hearty co-operation and proposed Alderman Forshaw as his successor, remarking that no member of the club had its interests more at heart,and was sure he would fill the office with every degree of pride. (Applause). Mr. James Richardson seconded, Mr. John Mainwaring supported, and the motion was carried. Alderman Forshaw, who had a hearty reception, thanked the members for the high honour they had conferred upon him. He was following in the line of a worthy list of predecessors, and hoped that his year of office would be associated with a most successful season. (Applause.) The retiring presi- dent was heartily thanked for his services. The following officials were appointed : —Hon. secretary, Mr. C. H. Unsworth : hon. treasurer, Mr. R. Silcock; ' assistant treasurer, Mr. R. H. Daintith ; match secre- tary, Mr. W. H. Podmore ; cricket, captain first team, Mr. J. W. Bibby; deputy captain, Mr. B. Silcock; second team captain, Mr. E. Pierpoint ; bowling. hon. secretary, Mr. Thomas Cooper; captain, Mr. R. T. Fairclough ; auditor, Alderman Tinnion. Committee— Cricket, Messrs. J. W. Bibby, B. Silcock, W. Richardson; bowling, Messrs. R. H. Daintith, J. Lee, B. Isaac and Pearson; tennis, Mr. R. Pierpoint. A motion to reduce the entrance fee to the Tennis Club from :£1 is. to 10s. 6d. was defeated. It was argued ithat few only took advantage of the courts, and the cost of the maintenance of the same did not warrant any reduction.

During the early part of the THE COST OF Parliamentary session esti- EDUCATION. mates of expenditure for the

public services crowd on us almost daily. Within the last few days we have had under consideration estimates for the Army and Navy, and now comes the Education Esti- mates. While the cost of armaments this year shows a reduction, the coat of education shows a steady increase year by year. Less than twenty years ago the annual cost to the Excheqner of public education for the whole of the United King- dom was about five and a-half millions. The esti- mates issued yesterday account for seventeen and a-half millions. This increase is stupendous, but it takes no account of a corresponding increase that has been going on throughout that period in the cost of education to local rates, amounting now to considerably more than ten millions a year, which brings the total annual expenditure on education to something nearly approaching thirty [ millions. That is to say, public education now costs the country nearly as much as either the Navy or the Army, and is certainly lmore than three times as expensive to the public as it was twenty years ago. It is for this reason that these etimates should be carefully scanned, for while nobody will 'deny to education any more than to the Navy and Army all the money necessary to guarantee efficiency, yet it is neeessary, where so much public money is involved, that we should be quite certain that none of it is wasted. These estimates are less than half a million more than last year, but that comes on the top of regular increases for many years. In England and Wales £54,100 extra is required for grants to public elementary schools and £lO,OOO for free grants. As there is no increase in the rate for either of these payments, the increases are entirely accounted for by the increase in the number of scholars in public elementary schools. There is also a huge increase in the amount set apart for building grants for training colleges, and there is, of course, ss corresponding increase in the amount for maintenance of these institutions. Grants to secondary, technical and evening schools have gone up to the tune of £127,000. There is also set apart a sum of £lOO,OOO for a purpose which has not previously appeared on the estimates— special provision for new schools. Notwithstanding these increased amounts, we venture to think that the House of Commons and the public generally will agree that they are necessary. The most urgent educational problem of the moment is the manufacture of good teachers and the increase of building and maintenance grants for training collegas is well -invested money. Especially is this the case when we remember that new regulations issued about nine months ago abolished building grants altogether for denomina- tional training colleges, but increased the amount payable to local authorities for this purpose from 25 to 75 per cent. of the actual cost. The institutions to which these new grants are paid will, at all events, be under public control. There will, too, be very little complaint of the increase in grants for secondary education pur- poses; but there is one matter in this connection which should not escape attention. While much debate centres round the question of public con- trol of elementary schools, we are rapidly estab- lishing a system of secondary education largely financed by public money with very little public control indeed. The Board of Education gives grants and all local authorities give subsidies to privately-managed institutions in thousands of cases, and this is a matter which should be raised when the estimates are considered. The hundred thousand pouuds which is ear-marked for building purposes is, of course, the direct out- come of the failure of last year's Bill, and indicates that Mr. MCKENNA means so to administer his Department as to encourage local authorities to close inefficient and insanitary voluntary schools by assisting them to build new Council schools. It is to be noted that there is nothing in these estimates for the relief of the education rate in the heavily-rated areas. S3mething was done in a tentative way last year, and it may be taken for certain that, although nothing is appropriated by the estimates for this purpose now, the local althorities which have rates for education ranging from is. 6d. to 3s. in the will bring pressure to bear on the Chancellor of the Exchequer before long.

The workingmen of War WORKINGMEN AND rington are to be eongratu- THE INFIRMARY. lated upon the happy eon-

summation ot theli- noble efforts on behalf of the Warrington Infirmary. For some years it has been their aim to increase their annual contributions to £l,OOO and at last this laudable ambition has been realized., All praise is due to the workers who have given according to their means to support this most worthy institu- tion. There was only one cause of regret at the annual meeting of the committee on Saturday and that was that the late respected chairman—Alder- man Roberts—had not lived to share in the gratification felt at the attainment of an object which was peculiarly dear to him. Although the sum of 5,1,000 is a handsome donation for which the Committee deserves full credit, we believe that with improved organization at some of the works,even better results will be forthcoming.lt was pointed out on Saturday that the employees of several firms were not by any means as generously represented in the subscription list as they should be. We feel sure that if these workers are appealed to and adequate arrangements made,they will not be behind their comrades in supporting an institution which is preeminently of value to the working classes. In view of the extension which has been embarked upon, the need for enhanced subscriptions is particularly urgent, and we hope the Board will succeed in their endeavour to open the enlarged premises free of debt.

CYCLES with Sturmey Archer three at the Church. ' speed gear, nine guineas. Two speed gear eight guineam.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 53, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Fatality at a Warrington Works. Mr. S. Brighouse held at an inquest at the Coroner's Court, Warrington, yesterday, on the body of Henry Cook, a married man, 39 years of age of 22, Norman- street, who was killed at Messrs. Moriics,Hall,and Co.'s works on Tuesday.—The evidence showed that on the afternoon in question deceased, with five other workmen, was engaged in the erection of some scaffolding at the new steel works of Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co., for the purpose of painting the roof. A large plank was being hoisted by means of a pulley block and rope, when the rope broke, and the plank fell a considerable distance on Cook's head, and he was instantly/ killed.— The Coroner examined the block and rope and said it was absolutely unfitted for the work, the rope being completely perished.—J. Ward, the foreman painter, said that the proper block required for that purpose was in use in another part of the works. The one used at the time of the accident, was not thoroughly examined, as it was thought to be all right.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental Death," and the foreman said they were of opinion that there had been a certain ambunt of neglect on the part of the firm in not having the ropes, etc., periodically examined.-1 The Coroner: Do you find there was a defective block.—The Foreman: Certainly —Mr. F. G. Taylor, on behalf of the firm.expressed regret at the accident. "A Holiday in Greece." There was a large attendance of members of the Warrington Society at the Old Academy on Thursday evening when Mr. F. R. Roberts delivered a lecture on "A. holiday in Greece and the Isles of Greece." Mr. E. J. Hall presided. The lecture was a highly interesting one and afforded a delightful evening to those who were privileged to hear it. Mr. Roberts vividly described his experiences during the Grecian tour which it was his good fortune to make last year when he witnessed the Olympian games at Athens. By a series of excellent lantern slides he was able to bring home to his hearers the beauties of Greece and its islands which have been rendered sacred by a multitude of historic associations. First: the ruins of Olympia, where the great religious and athletic festivals used to take place, were shown. Then the audience was transported in imagination to some of the picturesque Grecian isles and the views of the ancient temples and statuary, and ruins which I have survived from the age of the old mythology, I were particularly fine. One island —Delos—to which the people of neighbouring places had resort to pay their religious duties, was literally one mass of remains of temples and other sacred building Not of the Hellenic mythology alone are these islands witness, for some are rich in early Christian associa- tions The assembly saw with special interest glimpses of Patmos, where St. John was exiled, and one view depicted the cave in which tradition sap he lived. Hard by stands a monastery where Mr. Roberts I and party had the privilege of inspecting precious manuscripts. Many other places of great interest were described and illustrated, notably Athens with its majestic and graceful buildings. The scene when the Olympian games were commencing was also thrown upon the screen. The lecture must certainly rank as I one of the most enjoyable given before the Society,and the only regret of those present was that they too bad I not had the opportunity of touring in that beautiful ; region. A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. I Roberts on the proposition of Mr. Madeley seconded by I Mr. Beck. Mr. Frith ably manipulated the lantern. BABY CARRIAGES.—New and exclusive designs ; now ehowing.—T. S. G.A.RL AND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Animated Pictures at the Parr Hall. I Animated pictures rank amongst the most popular • forms of entertainment at present before the public, ' and the programme which is being presented at the Parr Hall by Mr. Sydney H. Carter's New Century • Company is exceptionally attractive. Pevious visits lof this exhibition to Warrington have gained for it a high reputation and the large audiences which have assembled every evening this this week have thoroughly enjoyed the up-to-date entertainment given under Mr. Arthur Northrop's capable management. Perhaps the best picture is the representition of the comicalities of Little Tich, showing that famous artiste in his most laughable and grotesque antics. There are a host of other humorous reproductions which create great merriment. The crude operations of the Village Fire Brigade, causing the fire to burn more furiously, are well depicted and the incident is appropriately closed with the arrival of the town brigade which comes upon the scene in dashing style and soon subdues the outbreak. Another notable series of pictures shows a force of policemen in valiant pursuit of a bull dog which in the end turns the tables and causes the. "men in blue" to, beat an unceremonious retreat. The audiences are convulsed witll the disastrous results of Mr. Jenkins' experiment at" washing-np " and the equally astonishing rapidity with which he repairs the havoc wrought. While the humorous element is sq, much to the fore,thare are many pictures appealing to other tastes. Fan-making in Japan is admirably depicted, and there are realistic I representations of a fox-hunt and a visit to the Zoo. IThe programme is not without its thrilling episodes, I the chief being a number of sensational happenings in the life of a cowboy. There are still plenty of opportunities for those of our readers who have not already seen this excellent entertainment to do so, as the Company's visit will extt,nd over the whole of next week and matin4es will be given each Saturday.

* * Half-measure, w th the House of Lords do not appeal ! to Mr. Silas K. Hocking, the novelist, who was one of the speakers .at the Free Church Congress on I Wednesday. He said the House of Lords wits corn- posed of antediluvian fossils, who breathed an atmos- phere in which freedom could not live: it was a House largely replenished by the plutocrats of the drink ring and the gutter press, a House buttressed by ! the heads of the Established Church, which had , remained-true to its predatory instincts and its hatred I of social reform. With the Parliament of the people they were generally well satisfied, but Samson was: shorn of his locks by that antiquated menagerie, the 1 House of Lords. (Laughter). "The first necessity of the age," he went on, `• is to take away the pestilentia carcase and cremate it."

Chemical Society's Visit to Crosfield's. The Chemical Society of the University of , Birmingham having intimated that they wished to make Messrs. Cresfield's works the objective of their annual excursion, permission was readily granted by the Directors, and as a result about 40 visited Bank Quay on Thursday afternoon. The visitors, who, in the unavoidable absence of Professor Frankland were led by Dr. Finlay, on arriving at the works were sub- , divided into small parties which, under the guidance 1, of various members of the laboratory staff, made a tour ' of the most interesting parts of the works including the general offices,laboratories,printing and lithographic departments, soap-stamping, box-making, and milling departments, Roapery, silicate,glycerine and engineer- ing departments. the various processes being briefly deseribed in each case by Mr. J. Allan. Subsequently the visitors were entertained to tea in the girls' dining room. At the conclusion Dr. Finlay in a very happily conceived little speech expressed the very great pleasure the members of the Society had derived from their .all too brief inspection of this varied and vastly interesting industry and on his own behalf and on behalf of his fellow members thanked the Directors for the cordiality of their reception, and their guides (Mr. Allan especially) for their extreme kindness and the pains they had taked in satisfying the visitors' curiosity. Mr. Allan briefly acknow- ledged the compliment on behalf of the firm. After inspecting the fire station the visitors left Bank Quay returning to Birmingham by special saloon on the 5.50'
