DEATH OF THE CHIEF CONSTABLE.

SAD NEWS FROM CAIRO.

We regret very much to annouw that Mr. Luke Talbot (Chief Constable of Warrington) died at Cairo yesterday. A cablegram conveying the sad news was received from the British Consul at Cairo last night. It is only a week or two since he was granted extended leave of absence, and at once departed for Egypt, where it was hoped that he would recover from the effects of the severe illness he had under- gone during the winter. At Tuesday's Council meet- ing reference was made to the absent officer, and it seems particularly sad that—as in the case of the late Medical Officer—death sinuld follow almost immedi- ately onthegranting of a holiday which was expected to restore health.

Mr. Talbot was a very popular ()Meer in Warrington and his untimely death will be widely regretted. He was born at Bradford in 1862 and spent his youth in the Southern and Western States of America, France and Portugal. In England too, his career was varied, for he tried press work and the stage before joining the police force in the capacity of constable. Here his promotion was rapid, ani after lively experiences as a detective he was appointed Chief Constable of Kendal at the age of 28. In 1895 he obtained the vacancy at Warringion, and at once affected a great improvement in the borough force by inaugurating a detective department. Various other reforms werl! initiated by him, and there is no doubt that the deceased officer had a great capacity for organisa- tion. As might be surmised Mr. Talbot was a man of many parts, and in addition to occasional literary efforts of considerable quality, he had a reputation as a good story-teller, athlete, and smut 'middle-weight boxer. The late Mr. Talbot leaves a widow, but no children.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

[By " Ovallst."] It is a remarkable thing how often Fate has drawn Warrington and Batley together in cup-ties, and the association of the two clubs in such serious rivalry still continues. The draw tho week has resulted in Warrington and Batley being pitted together for at least the fourth time in recent years, and for the second year in succession. So far the "Gallant Youths" have triumphed every time, but I think the day of their humiliation is at hand. This is the first occasion on which they have been tied to visit Wilders- pool in the Cup Competition, and they will probably return sadder and wiser men after next Saturday's game. Even were the match at Batley I should have confidence in the Warrington men, though they lost by a. point 12 months ago. At home, however, they will have complete confidence in their ability to give Batley a sound thrashing, and that confidence ;will be fully shared by all their supporters.

The draw for the first round of the cup-ties is as 'ollocvs : Swinton v. St. Helens. Liverpool City v. Hull. Wigan v. Hunslet. Whitehaven Recreation v. Saville Green. Hull Kingston Rovers v. Bradford. Oldham v. Runcorn. Salford v. Leigh. Workington or Maryport v. Wakefield Trinity. Broughton Rangers v. Widnes. Huddersfield v. Brighouse St. James's. Leeds v. Rochdale Hornets. Millom v. Halifax. Warrington v. Batley. Barrow v. Dewsbury. Radcliffe Rangers v. York or Newington Rovers. Keighley v. Brookland Rovers.

The first thing which strikes one about the draw is the good luck of the Lancashire clubs. For county purposes Runcorn are regarded as being in Lanca shire, but leaving the Cheshire club out, the next- eight clubs belonging to the County Palatine are Wigan, Oldham, Warrington, Salford, Broughton Rangers, Barrow, and Swinton, and all of them have been drawn at home.

Warrington supporters witnessed an exhilarating match at Wilderspopl on Saturday when Broughton Rangers were the visitors. Previously the Rangers had beaten Warrington in the League watch at Broughton, and in the Lancashire Cup Final at Wigan, so that the homesters had something to wipe off the slate. They succeeded acoording to expectations and Broughton were well beaten by 12 points to 5. It was a somewhat desperate struggle and neither side could afford to relax their efforts for a moment. Warrington came back into something like their early season form, and their attacking movements were full of dash, but the combination still leaves a lot to be desired. Broughton are also below their best form, but never- theless the game was full interest and excitement from beginning to end.

The adjourned Licensing Sessions for the Borough were held at the Police Court. yesterday. Roberts, H. Thornton, G. Pilling, and A. J. Jolley. OFF-LICENCES.

At the annual sessions the question of the renewal of the off-licences in the Borough was adjourned. Yester- day the Mayor announced that these would be renewed this year but that in all probability there would be some reduction in the number next year. BILLIARD SALOON LICENCE GRANTED. _

Mr. Browne renewed his application on behalf of 11Ir. Metcalf, Liverpool, for a billiard licence for the Jubilee Buildings, Horsemerket-street, in which it was proposed to place 14 tables. Mr. Browne pointed out that when he originally made the application the only objection which was made to it was that the entrance to the Catholic Young Men's Club and to the first floor of the proposed saloon were by the same stairway. This had now been altered and an entrance to the Young Men's Club constructed in Lyine-streei, the entrance to the saloon being from Florsemarket-street. The respec- tive premises were divided by fire-proof partitions. Mr. Hallaway, deputy town clerk, offered no objec- tion to the granting of the licence, provided Mr. Metcalf undertook to close the premises every night at eleven o'clock, and not to allow pool, pyramids or handicaps to be played. 1 Mr. Browne gave the required undertaking, and with- out retiring the magistrates granted the application.

The opposition of the WARRINGTIN &ND Corporation of W irrinitton THE RIVER. and certain traders to the

present Ship Canal Bill for • additional powers, has been unsuccessful. It is • essential that Warr.ngton's interests as effected by the river should be carefully preserved, and the proper course was taken in opposing the Canal -"Company's Bill and endeavouring to get the nrotective clauses which were deemed necessary. The main point was that the proposed nurnuirig by the Company of a large volume of water from the river estuary to the upper reaches of the Canal would increase the salinity of the river Water through the connection at Woolston Weir. "Unfortunately the Ehuse of Lords Committee made light of Warrington's objections on this score, refusing to admit either that the water was likely to be made salty, or if so that the eff4ot Upon boilers and manufacturing processes would be serious. The trarters who use a great quantity of river water view the contingency with alarm, and it is at; least significant that the Canal Company, while pooh-poohing the danger, refused to agree to a Protective clause being granted t., Warrington. The C wnoration has, at any rate, succeeded in securini, that no more water shall he abstracted from the 'inner pert of the river then Was sanctioned by the Amt of 18S.1. and has also: safegnarded,the pudic rights in the footpath along Arpley meadows. The question of the possible contamination of the river water will have to be 'further considered by the traders.

We think we may safely • THE TOWN COUNCIL. give the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) redit fnr the better tone which is apparent in Town Council debates to-day as con,pared with the last few years. There is much less bickering and interrupt- ing, and the improvement is due to the firm - ,ness, impartiality and tact of the 3rayor. He has the personal recommendation of keep- ing "a still tongue," which is, of course, Proper procedure for a chairman to observe. At Tuesday's meeting there was two full hours discussion. but no quarrelling or exhibitions of temper. Even Councillor Pemberton gets a fair hearing now-a-days and it is noticeable how much less he says when he is allowed to speak without interruption and retort. The business of this week's meeting was not of great woment,and there Was not a single division, or amendfnent put during the evening. There were several matters of interest,notablv the proposition of Alderman Evans that the widening of Church-street on the south -side should be proceeded with.TheConncil was in a Complaisant mood till it was pointed mit that the •scheme would involve exaenditure chargeable on the rates for one year of two or three thousand Pounds. Alderman Evans's proposal did not give any particulars RS to coat, and as it was ruled to be mandatory if passed, the Alderman eventually Withdrew it. Meanwhile we expect that a report Will be drawn up and submitted to the Council showing exactly what is required in Church-street and what it will coat. There is not much pro- bability that any extensive expenditure will he sanctioned there for some time. As Councillor lird pointed out, Bridge-street is not finished yet and, moreover, the Howley end of the town has done very well lately in.secnring special favours. By wav,what has become of that beautiful scheme for Howard Place, the site of the old Bridewell ?

Lieutenant- Colonel THE SUICIDE Unsworth, chief of the BUREAU. Salvation Army's Suicide Bureau, formed at the beginning of this year for the purpose of lessening the number of suicides by inviting those con- templating belf-destruction to seek advice and ass.tstanoe, has reported to Mr. Bramwell Booth, rchief of the staff, on the operations of the bureau Or the two months of its existence. The report is of. Et very encouraging character, but it might be said that the bureau has proved only too success- NI in that it has demonstrated how large a number of unfortunates contemplate suicide as the only g.Tnean'l of getting rid ot their troubles. During the .Lygo in.onths 600 cases were dealt with either by Tterviewe or by letters, and Lieutenant-Colonel Unsworth states that a condition of suffering has been. thereby revealed such as perhaps the most nesaimistie of the community never dreamed of. The cases dealt with are classified by the officer in charge of the bureau as follows : —Lonely class,so; victims of money troubles, 3:50; persons criminally involved. 50: victims of the morphia end drug habits, 80. In several cases of money troubles, serious embezzlement was admitted, and the offenders were induced to make confession and restitution as far as possible. Two men took their lives without waiting for the answers to the communications they had addressed to the bureau. It is quite evident, however, that th suicide bureau is an institution which was sorely needed, and which will certainly do much to reduce the appalling number of suicides taking place in the country every year. People seem to be Retting more and more indifferent to the moral wrong in taking their own lives. The temptation to play the coward's part and end all by one mad ect is strong, and the Salvation Army by offering these miserable beings a gleam of hope through Its bureau, has accomplished a piece of social reformation second to none in the grand record of achievements behind it.

Egerton-Warburton, J Bolshaw, T. Antwis, J. H. Davies, W. Weir, P. Mounfleld, J. Whitley, J. Spruce, H. Eyes, C. Roylance, J. Hallows, J. B. Chadwick. T. Wright, S. Woodward, G. F. Ashton (clerk). W. The County Council forwarded a list of recommenda- tions of action to be taken with the object or diminish- ing the excessive number of deaths amongst young children, and asked the Council to adopt them. The recommendations included full inquiry, into the death of every infant; they also suggested more stringent regulations in regard to mothers working in factories, building bye-laws, hospital provision, sanitation, and instructions to mothers in respect to feeding and care of infants, by means of tracts, and the regulation of dairies. Mr. Woodward weed that a number of the regula- tions were not applicable to the rural districts. Dr. Adams was instructed to prepare a report on the recommendations. - - -

THE WIDNES SILVER CASE.—AN A.CQUITTAL. At Liverpool Assizes on Saturday Michael Riley (34), foreman, was on his trial for the third day charged with having, at Widnes, stolen severel pounds weight of silver bullion of the value of £22, the property of James H. Dennis and Co., Limited. He was also charged with having feloniously received the silver. Mr. Rigby Swift and Mr. Kennedy again repre- sented the prosecution, and Mr. H. L. Riley and Mr. Singleton were for the defence.—The allegation against the prisoner was that whilst acting as foreman copper smelter at the works of the prosecutors in Widnes he unlawfully obtained possession of several pieces of silver—which was a by-product in the manufacture of sulphate of copper—and that he sent an ex-pawnbroker's assistant named Carroll to Liverpool to dispose of them, prisoner paying Carroll's railway fare. Carroll went to a jeweller's ;hop in the city on Wednesday, January 3, and produced two packages,which contained silver, but the contents of which Carroll afterwards said he was not aware of. The jeweller arranged for an appointment on the following days but when Carroll re-appeared on Thursday he was arrested. A statement which he made accusing Riley led to the arrest of the latter and to his own release. Riley pro- tested that he had not given the silver to Carroll, and that he had not seen Carroll since the Tuesday. Evidence,however,was given on behalf of the prosecutor to show that prisoner and Carroll had been together in a public-house on the Wednesday—the day on which Carrot was alleged to have been to Liverpool with silver. —The morning was devoted to the closing speeches.— Mr. Rigby Swift, anticipating one of the points for the defence, asked the jury to take the view that Carroll had given his evidence in such a straightforward way that he ought to receive the same respectful credence as though he were any other witness—notwithstanding the fact that he had himself been convicted of obtain- ing goods by fraud. His (Carroll's) story differed in no material particular form from that which he had given in the police conrt.—Mr. Riley, for the defence, said that prior to those proceedings there had never been a breath of suspicion against his client. He was a man of excellent character, and he had given entire satisfaction not only to his employers at Widnes, but to those who had engaged him to lay down new works in France and South Wales. Counsel urged that Carroll's evidence was that of an accom- plice, but, in addition to that, the evidence was almost wholly circumstantial. The jury ought not to convict on the unsupported e uidence of an accompl ice.— The Judge, in his summing up, pointed out that the prisoner was a man of blameless character, and that although his house had been searched by the police no incriminating evidence of a wicked act was forthcoming —so far as that search itself was concerned. His lord- ship laid stress on the fact that it was not for counsel for the defence to prove that prisoner was innocent, but for counsel for the prosecution to show that he was guilty. "We bear of miscarriages of justice now and again," said his lordship. " but I haven't seen one such case. so far as I am concernedwhilstl have been a judge, and I believe that if that simple principle was attended to there would be extremely few miscarriages of justice." Headvised the jury that it was not safe to rely upon the evidence of an accomplice unless it was corroborated in some material particllars.—Tie jury found the prisoner not guilty, and he was discharged.

ST. HELENS.

SUNDAY GAMBLERS FINED.

At St. Helens on Monday, John Lee, a middle-aged man, of 187, Park-road, was charged with gambling. Police-constable Whalley said that he and Police- constable Anders were concealed on the St. Helens Town Football Ground on Sunday afternoon and saw a gang of over twenty men and youths gambling. They caught Lee and Edward Kay, of 91 Park-road, and Lee was detained for the night.—Lee was fined £'.l 45., or tv-enty-eight days, and Kay ss. and costs. ST HELENS BUILDER'S TRAGIC DEATH. __ • _ _ _ _

Mr. Brighouse held an inquiry at St. Helens on Mon- day into the death of Arthur William Ellison, builder, aged thirty-one years,lf 173, Boundary-road, who was found drowned on Friday last.—Mrs. Ellison gave evidence, and said that deceased with his tvko brothers were executors under the will of his father, and they carried on the business of builders. Deceased was of a quiet, retiring nature, and his home life was a very happy one. Last Christmas he had a slight attack o f influenza, and had since suffered from insomnia. He had occasionally complained of severe pains in the head. On Nlonday last week he collected rents at Sutton Heath, Gerrard's-bridges, and Ashton-in-Mai or- field. He returned to the office about five o'clock and balanced up with his brothers,an d afterwards went home. On arriving home his wife asked him if he was going to have his tea, when he said. "I am not particular. I am about tired of life, and I think I will do away with myself." Later he worked at his books and conversed with his wife at intervals. About five minutes to ten o'clock he went to the front door to smoke a cigarette and then disappeared. Police-constable Wilkes spoke to the body being recovered by Captain Byefield of Runcorn on Friday afternoon last, in the Alexandra dam, off Prescot-road. Deceased's brother, Thomas Ellison, said that beyond being slightly absent-minded recently, he saw nothing wrong with his brother up to Monday last week. On the day he disappeared he put everything in perfect order. His books were beautifully kept. There was absolutely nothing in his business or in his private life to account for his taking away his life.—The jury returned a verdict of " Suicide whilst of unsound mind," and expressed their deepest sympathy with the wife and relatives, in which the coroner con- curred.

ST. HELENS PHOTOGRAPHIC EXHIBITION.

The sixth annual exhibition of pictorial photography in connection with the St. Helens Camera Club is being held this week at the Windle Pilkington Hall at the Y.M.C.A. The exhibition was opened on Monday evening by Colonel W. W. Pilkington. The number of pictures is about the same as last year. The show is open to clubs in the Lancashire and Cheshire Photographic Union,but the open classes have not been well supported. The majority of the 200 frames are the work of the members of the St. Helens clubs, and many of the pictures have been entered for the forthcoming Northern ehotographic Exhibition at Liverpool. The judging was carried out on Saturday by Dr. Thurston Holland, F.R.P.S., Liverpool, and Mr. T. Lee Syms,of Tyldesley. The pictorial character of the work is of a high order, and marks the remarkable progress that the club has made during the past four years. In the landscape, figure study, and architectural classes great strides have been made, and the most critical selection committee would find It difficult to object to any of the pictures being admitted to the most up-to-date competitive exhibition. Mr. W. IT, Ward carries off the first prize with "A Grey Morn," which is an excellent example of bromide work, but the success is mainly due to the originality of the composition and point of view. Mr. J. Hesford is second with a really charming representa- tion of " The Silent Eventide." Mr. Hesford is first in the still-life class, as well as for figure studies. His "Snowstorm," which wins in the latter class, last week came out first in one of the great public competitions organised by the photographic Press. It is a skilful rendering of children forcing their way through the blinding snow, and the driving snow and cheerless suroandings are splendidly realised. Mr. A. M. Poole, one of the club's ablest and most prolific workers' shows several carbon prints of undoubted merit, and Mr. Poole scores in the architec- ture and postcard classes. The president (Mr. J. Collins), has confined his attention chiefly to figure studies, and shows several interesting pictures. Mr. J. Glover, tbe secretary, entered in the open class, and his " November " only fell a very few marks behind the first award. Mr. H. Gee and Mr. H. Waizbom lead the younger members, and excellent work is shown by Messrs. H. R. Lacy, A. Marsden, W. Inman, J. Leyland, J. Donnellan, G, H. Alban, J. T. Elliott, A bcott, T. T. Best, W. Williams, and J. T. Gregory. During the week special lantern lectures have been given in the gymnasium Architecture: 1, " Tintern," A. M. Poole ; 2 (and hon. mention), "Doorway, Lincoln," H. R. Lacey. Portraiture and Figure Studies : 1, "The Snowstorm," J. Hesford; 2, " A Lancashie Lad," A. M. Poole; hon. mention, "A Quiet Hour," J. Leyland; hon. mention, "In Mem.", J. Hesford. Landscape, Seascape, &o. : 1, "A Grey Morn," W. H. Ward; 2' "The Silent Even- tide," J. Hesford ; 3, " Morning Mists," W. Inman ; 4, 1" Farmer's Pride," A. M. INCREASING DEMAND FOR AMERICAN TINNED MEATS.

In a recent letter addressed to the Trade papers, Mr. Geo. Little, the well-known Provision Merchant of Manchester, states that the trade in American Tinned Meats is slowly but surely being regained. He calls attention to the fact that the British pablic have recognised the weakness of the wild tiradft made by sensational writers against a square and honest industry, and shows that the improved methods of production which result in a perfect article should commend Tinned Meat as a wholesome food for the daily consumption of the British people: -in fact it may be truthfully called the diet of the nation. Cheese which used to be bought in the shop at 6d, per pound is now selling at 9s. and 10d. Corned beef, well-known for its nutritious value, sells at only Gd. per pound.

WARRINGTON I.L.P. DEBATING SOCIETY.

Paper on " Protection"

There was a good attendance at the weekly meeting -of the above debating Society on Wednesday evening, when Mr. G. H. W. Steel delivered a paper, his sub- ject being" Some reasons why lam Protectionist." At the commencement of his paper Mr. Steel said that he was not a protectionist because he was a Conservative. He said that in his opinion the question should never have been a party question. It was a national question said a question worthy of great consideration. The two principal objects of all protectionists were first the maintenance and the increase of the national strength -and the prosperity of the United Kingdom and in the second place the realisation of the greatest ideal which has ever come to any statesmen— to cement the union of the States beyond the seas and to consolidate the British race. Mr. Steel then pointed out that although in 30 odd years trade bad only increased 7i per cent. the population of the British Isles had increasebd 30 per cent. The actual increase in the British Isles, the great Free :trade Country, was only 20 millions in 30 odd years whilst in U.S.A. and Germany trade had increased during the same time to the extent of 110 millions and 56 millions respectively. Continuing the lecturer said that in the time of Mr. Cobden we were without dispute the work- shop of the world. In his days foreign countries supplied us with our foods and raw materials whilst we in return sent them our manufactured goods. Compar- ing our trade with the Colonies and foreign countries he stated that our trade with foreign countries had declined to the extent of 42 millions whilst our trade with British possessions liad increased 40 millions. Be contended that these figures proved beyond doubt that our Imperial trade was absolutely essential to the Prosperity of the country. If that trade declined along With foreign countries, and did not increase in propor- tion to our population our country would sink at once into a fifth-rate power. The essayist contended that our colonies bad more foresight than the Mother Country, for they had adopted the policy of Protection. He could not understand how anyone professing to be a Trade Unionist could make the views of Free Trade coincide with their trade union views. Free Traders said we must buy in the cheapest market, and people who said that in regard to communities would say the same when it came to individuals. If they could get a non-union man to work for Bd. an hour and a trade union man wanted 10d. they would at once use the same argument and say that we must buy in the cheapest market. Mr. Steel said that a great argument used at the last election was that Protection would be the cause of the formation of trusts and combines. They had had an examplethat combines could be formed even under Free Trade and an attempt had been made to form a great combine by the individuals who had corn- demned the principles of trusts. The paper, which lasted over half an hour,was listened to with attention and a very spirited debate followed in which the following gentlemen took part :—Messrs. Hill, Haley, Randles, Ellwell, Phillips, Walker, Lees, Roscoe, Lazenby and Councillor Stott. Mr. Charles Lee presided over the meeting and seconded the vote of thanks to the lecturer which was proposed by Mr. . alker, the Secretary of the Debating Society.

THE INFIRMARY. WORKINGMEN CONTRIBUTE £1,003

The Workingmen's Committee of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary has had the satisfaction of realizing an ambition long entertained, for during the past year the members have succeeded in raising £l,OOO on behalf of the institution. This gratifying result of the committee's work was the subject of con- gratulation at the annual meeting which was held at the Town Hall on Saturday afternoon. The Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) presided over a large attendance of representatives of the various works, and was sup- ported by Councillor Burton (chairman of the com- mittee), Mr. W. Peter Rylands, Mr. George Thompson, Revs. W. Bracecamp and J. P. Whittle, Mr. J. FL J. Hampson (hon. treasurer), and Mr. Reuben Higharn (hon. secretary). Lette:s of apology for non- attendance were received from the Rev. John Yonge, Mr. John Fairclough, Mr. C. Jackson Holmes and Mr. F. Monks. The last-named gentleman wrote that he was sorry not to be present to congratulate the com- mittee on accomplishing the task he set them many years ago, of raising £l,OOO a year for the benefit of the Infirmary. There was plenty of rciom for a further increase in the amount, if all the works were efficiently organized and all the men were willing to fall in with the weekly payment system. The MAYOR said he was very pleased that the workingmen took up so admirably the object of pro- viding an institution for the sick and needy. No one knew the amount of good the Infirmary did, but they often heard expressions of gratitude for the help and kindness received there. It was the poor of the town to whom the institution was the greatest boon. He was glad the Committee had taken a decided step for- ward in embarking upon the extension scheme, which had become very necessary. The Infirmary and similar institutions had his grratest sympathy. Years ago he made up his mind that if it lay in his power to help the Infirmary and Nurses' Home he would certainly do so, and as far as his means went he would support them. (Applause). He hoped they would all live to see the opening of the extension free from debt. (Applause.) If their old friend the late Alderman Roberts had been alive, that day would have been one of the proudest of his life, for the working men had managed to raise during the past year just over £l,OOO. (Applause). Mr. HAMPSoN submitted the statement of accounts which showed that the amount raised at the works and places of business was £B6l 9s. 11d as against £Bl4 Is last year. There were other receipts from the concert, police sports &c., which brought the total to £1,012. 19s. 4d. After deducting expenses, the cash handed to the treasurer of the Infirmary was £l,OOO 6s id. as com- pared with £945 9s. sd. last year. Mr. Hampson expressed his pleasure that the work of the committee had gone ahead rapidly during the last few years. (Applause.) Councillor BURTON said the balance sheet was highly satisfactory, especially when they considered the counter-calls for subscriptions which had been made upon the workingmen. They had responded in a way which did them credit. Special thanks were due to Mr. Hampson for the keen interest tie bad taken in the work. They all simerely deplored the loss of such a valued friend and worker as their late chairman. Coun- cillor Burton then submitted some interesting figures with reference to the work of the Infirmary, showing that the total number of patients treated during the year was 9,000. The ordinary expenditure was £3,108 and the tncome £3,134. he employees of works had contributed to the extension fund £677 14s. 7d. in addition to the £lOOO raised for the general fond. (Applause.) Councillor Burton spoke of the excellent way in which the patients were attended to, and said many letters of thanks were received by the Board.

- . . The statement was adopted on the proposition of Mr. L. WEIn seconded by Mr. .1. W. TAYLOR. Mr. W. PV.TER. RYLANDS then delivered an interesting address on the extension scheme and said it was his great ambition to falsify the prophecies of some people by opening the extension absolutely free of debt. (Applause). He traced the history of the Infirmary; pointing out that they would shortly be celebrating its centenary, and also dealt with the need for the exten- sion. Comparing Warrington's position with forty other towns, they found that the average of infirmary accommodation for those places was 1.3 beds per 1,000 of the population, but Warrington had the peculiar distinction of being at the bottom of the list with half abed per 1,000 of the population. Wha4.,ever might be the cost, this stigma on the town must be removed and the real necessities of the workingpeople recognised. He believed the plans of the new building were absolutely the last word in Ififirmary construction. (Applause.) He did not think that in these days workingmen were disposed to take the view that they were objects of charity, but that it would be their pride to feel that the benefits they received were benefits to which they themselves contributed. While he welcomed the past year's contribution of £l,OOO he regretted that it did not appear to be given in reasonably equal proportions. Certain firms contributed genernnsly, while there were others whose subscriptions were utterly unworthy of them. He suggested improved organization as a means of increasing the contributions from the latter. The whole of the amount subscribed by works employees to the extension fund emanated from ten firms only. Those firms had given or promised £O7l and in addition their annual subscriptions amounted to £3OO. If all the other firms did the same the result would be a donation of £3,000 to the extension fund.. (Applause.) On the proposition of Mr. T. WADSWORTH, seconded by Mr. G. THOMPSON, Councillor Burton was re-elected Chairman of the committee. . -

Mr. George Walker was appointed deputy-chairman, Mr. Higbam secretary, and Mr. Hampson treasurer, praise being given to each gentleman for his valuable services. Mr. S. ROBERTS, a working man, alluded to the timo when he was a patient at the Infirmary, owing to his illness from aPthrax. He spoke in warm terms of the services of the doctors and nurses, and said that if he had been the King his treatment could not have been better. His case was, he said, the third which had occurred in 30 years. Votes of thanks were accorded to the Chairman, the Deputy-Chairman, the Secretary, the Treasurer, the Hen. Auditor, the Representative to the Education Committee, the Press. the Chief Constable and the Police, the Warrington Rugby Football Club, the Com- mittee of May Day Procession of Horses, Mr. Down's Band, the Hon. Medical Otlicers, Senior and Junior House Surgeons, Matron and nurses, and to his Worship the Mayor for presiding, and also for the use of the Council Chamber. _ _ .

Mr. C. M. Watkins and Mr. W. Daintith were appointed members of the Board of Management.

WORK OF THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

Mr. GEORG a WALKER, the representative to the Education Committee, in submitting his fourth annual report on the work of the Education Committee, said during the month the first Council provided school, the " Beamont " School, would be opened to receive scholars. The Board of Education were asked to per- mit the charging of school fees In that school. Per- mission was refused, a decision which they confirmed when asked to reconsider the matter. He agreed with that decision. The trend of public opinion was entirely ' in. favour of free elementary education, and to have per- mitted the charging of school fees in that first Council school would have intensely aggravated an already existing anomaly. Still, although in favour of free schools, he din not think the present an opportune time for adopting the policy generally in this town. The Education Committee estimate of expenditure for the coming year showed an increase equal to a rate of 2s. in the £. It was not a very gratifying feature for the ratepayer or the rentpayer, and was accounted for , partially by the opening and staffing of the" Beamont " School, but more largely by the annual increase of salaries to the teaching and administrative staff. Mr. Walker commented at length on these and other sub- jects connected with education, and, in conclusion, said it was a reflection on our school system that we had children in training from four to 14 years of age, practically ten years, and yet at the end of that time how unfitted they were for commencing at least their life's work. If the primary object of elemenary school teaching was the formation of character, how was it that the great majority of their youths were so thoughtless, so callous and utterly indifferent to self improvement? That was a generally admitted fact. One of the problemslwhich appeared to need solution was, why so few of their boys took kindly to the teach- ing profession. It held out to them far better financial propects, immediate and prospective, and better social conditions than were possible by acquiring a trade or devoting themselves to commercial office work. The hours of duty were not long, whilst the holidays, rest days and off-days were almost a by-word, and yet the profession failed to induce their boys to enter, and it was in danger of falling entirely into the hands of the female sex. That was recognized not only in this i country but was an admitted fact n America. (Hear, hear). He was of opinion that too many snbjects were attempted in the schools. They were always wisely selected and not enough time given for the scholar to digest what was taught. (Applause.) Advantage would have to be taken of the facilites placed by this town at the command of its youth, but two lessons needed to be taught and learned :.(1) There is dignity in labour; (2) We play Wilco and not live to play. (Applause.)

Withdrawals may take place up to the 12th inst. of the proceedings. It seemed a very lcng interval until the Chairman declared time to be up. No sooner had he done this than Mr. T. Williams rose and demanded a poll. It was ruled that he was too late, and the meeting at once divided into two sections, the majority upholding the Clerk's ruling, and the minority urging that as Mr. Williams was on his feet before the Chairman spoke, he was in time. Mr. Bradshaw expressed regret at the incident, but declined to alter his ruling, and with an expression of dissatisfaction Mr. Williams resumed his seat. PARISH AFFAIRS DISCUSSED. So far, as above indicated, the meeting had been of a most harmonious character, but a hen those present were invited to discuss parish business, as one gentle- man put it, it was expected "that the fun woula begin," and that those who had served the public during the past three years, would be severely heckled. Again was the popular opinion falsified, and as the proceedings opened so did they close, no hostile criticism being offered. Mr. J. W. Bouchier by reason of his position as chairman of the Pariish Council took over the control of the meeting and briefly but ably reviewed the most important work of the Council and Parochial Com- mittee during the past term of office. The first matter to be touched upon was the much vexed question of the paving of streets and back passages many of these being admittedly in a disgrace- ful condition. The onus of the blame for the delay was rightly thrown upon the Local Government Board but satisfaction was felt when Mr. Bouchier was able to announce that before long the whole of the work would be put in hand. Sanitary matters were briefly referred to and the inducements offered to property owners to make improvements were explained, but as in general the village sanitarily is in an excellent position no comment, beyond expressions of agreement, was made. At a recent Parish meeting, it will be remembered a somewhat heated discussion arose upon the proposal of the County Education Authority to erect a school to accommodate 800 children, in the village. Opponents of the scheme, and they proved to be in the majority, protested and petitioned against the proposal on the ground that the school was larger than was required to meet the requirements of the district. The wisdom of their action has been shown by the decision of the Education Committee to build a school to accommodate only 500, provided certain alterations are made to the existing schools. Mr. Bouchier then explained that the question of payment for the school arose, as the new building would serve several parishes, children coming from Latchford Without, Warrington and Walton, and said that someone would have to watch closely to see that Stockton Heath was not to heavily burdened. As the remark implied that the Council would do the watching it was heartily applauded. Mr. Bouchier in conclusion referred to the bye-laws and said that the Council had under consideration the model bye-laws of the Local Government Board with a view to adopting the most useful. Councillor Lord moved that a meeting of parishioners be held every six months as bethought it desirable that matters should be discussed more frequently than once a year. Councillor Pemberton seconded and referring to the by-law urged that they had not been uniformly administered, and hoped that the new laws would be such that they could make all builders conform to them. He believed in equality and justice. In answer to an interjection by Mr. Skelton he said he did not pretend to stand alone in that respect and, proceeding, pointed out that in Warrington before certain regulations were adopted in regard to plumbing, the plumbers were approached with the result that by-laws were adopted which satisfied both sides. He hoped something similar would be done in Stockton Heath. The motion was approved, after Mr. H. J. Westbrook had denied the impeachment that the bye-laws had not been uniformly administered. Other suggestions were thrown out and under the category of business done was the adoption of two resolutions one "to approach the Manchester Ship Canal Company to see if the unsightly hoardings by the swing bridge could not be removed," and the other " that the Parish Council should consider the advisibility of pro- viding a recreation ground for the children. The latter is not a new suggestion but judging from the feeling of Monday's meeting it is likely, to be favourably considered. GREAT SANKiY.

POLL TO TAKE PLACE. The proceedings at Great Sankey were hardly as harmonious as those in the Cheshire village, a poll being demanded in consequence of the meeting not being satisfied with the vote by show of hands. Mr. G. Thompson presided over a large attendance. Six gentlemen constitute the Parish Council and as more than this number were nominated the meeting proceeded in the usual course to vote upon the names, but when the result was declared—Messrs. Birch, Fairclough, Furness, Ireland, Taylor and Walters receiving the majority of votes,—Mr. Kilshaw, who had an extensive backing, demanded a poll. Unless by withdrawals the number of candidates is reduced to six, the election will take place on March 25th. , Parish affairs were then discussed and the Chairman announced that it had been decided to build a police station in the village.As this has for sometime been the subject of considerable agitation, the announcement was received with gratification. Complaints were made about the inconvenience caused by the closing of the Bridge over the canal at Sankey Bridges, but the chair- man explained that negotiations wbre in progress between the interested authorities, with a view to enlarging the bridge and effecting other improvements. Much regret was felt at the announcement that owing to ill health Mr. Thompson was retiring from public life, and the meeting accorded him a hearty vote of thanks for past services. WARRINGTON RURAL DISFRICT COUNCIL.

The Lighting of Winwiek Road.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday, when there were present :—Mr. A. Brooks (presiding). Dr. Murray, Messrs. G. Mather, G. Thompson, J. W Knowles, P. Longton, W. Houghton, J. Backbouse, J. C. Sutton, (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), H. H. Burnage (assistant surveyor), %V. Kerfout (inspector).

===== 0002601_19070309_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RATES.

The CLERK presented his estimate of expenditure for the half year ending September 31st. Lie was doubtful whether to have a 4d. or sd. rate and ultimately decided to divide the two and fix the rate at 40. The special expenses in the various parishes were: Burtonwood .280, Penketh £lOO, Poulton £5O, Rixton 250, Great Sankey £3O. The figures were all reduced. In those parishes in which there were contests for Parish or District Council the cost of the elections would fall as a special charge upon the parish, and the figures he had given would probably be too low.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LIGHTING OF WINWICK-ROAD.

The question of the lighting of the Winwick main , road from Longford Bridge to Winwick Church was again considered. At the last meeting of the Council the Clerk read letters from the Winwick Parish Council and the overseers for Winwick protesting against the proposal. On Wednesday the Clerk read a resolution unanimouslyapproved at the annual Parish meeting for Winwick, requesting the Council to postpone the ques- tion for the present. On the other side he had received a letter from the committee of visitors to the Winwick Asylum asking that the work should be proceeded with at an early date. Mr. KNOWLES said he looked at the question from a different point of view from that adopted by the Winwick Parish Council. At the Winwick Asylum they had from 200 to 300 resident attendants whilst s great many lived off the premises, and there was a great deal of traffic along the roadway in the shape of friends going to visit patients—(Mr. Longton : "Not at night time ')—and he had received more than one complaint about the road being frequented by undesirable charac- ters. The lighting of the road would largely do away with this, and make the thoroughfare more respectable than at present. When they looked at the other parishes they found that the Manchester-road district, Sankey and Penketh were well lighted, whilst Winwick was not. For the present he would only follow the lino of the Corporation gas main to the Waterworks, terminating with a lamp near the Post Office. When the Asylum was about to be built, he understood, there was a kind of undertaking given that the road should be lighted. Mr. LONGTON : I would like to see the undertaking. I have never heard of it before and I was on the Com- mittee.

Mr. KNowLEs : There was an understanding with the asylum. Continuing he said the question was then deferred in consequence of the South Lancashire Tram- way Company offering to light the road from Newton to Warrington in consideration of their being allowed to use the road.

Mr. NEWCOMBE, in answer to Mr. Knowles, said 25 lamps would be required to do the lighting sug- gested. These would cost £2 13s. 4d. each per annum. The CHAIRMAN: About ld. in the .E on the rateable value.

Mr. KNOwLES : It has been said in Winsvick that it would cost 6d. in the

Mr. SUTTON explained that the Asylum authorities would have to bear the greater proportion of the cost hS farmers were only called upon to pay one quarter of the ratm.

Mr. KNOWLES explained that out of an annual cost o £6O, the Asylum would have to pay 240. He moved that the work be proceeded'tsith. Mr. SUTTON said that a few years ago the question of lighting the Winwick road was being favourably considered by the Council. The subject was, however, deferred in view of legislative powers being sought by the Lancashire Tramways Company, which if carried out would have fixed upon the company the liability for the efficient lighting of the road. He did not remember any particular arrangement with the Asylum people about the lighting. Mr. LoNoToN said he did not think the Winwick people objected to the small scheme proposed by Mr. Knowles, but they regarded it as the thin edge of the wedge, as the lighting was a great deal more needed in other parts of the district. Orford wanted lighting as well, and before they had finished they would be landed in a big scheme. What they objected to more than anything else was the discourteous manner in which the question had been raised. Mr. Knowles had gone over the heads of the Parish Council, and they objected to any scheme until it came from the Parish Council. In a very short time Winwick would be prepared to bring a scheme forward themselves. Mr. THOMPSON said he felt sure that when the work was done everyone would be pleased. He seconded Mr. Knowles' motion. _ _

Mr. HOUGHTON supported the motion on the ground that the improvement would be of great benefit to the general community. Mr. MATHER thought the Council should have more regard to the wishes of the Winw ick people. Upon a vote being taken Messrs. Mather, Backhouse, and Longton voted for the postponement of the question and Messrs. Knowles, Houghton and Thompson for the motion.

As Mr. Longton declined to consent to an adjourn- ment of less than six months the Chairman voted for the motion.

The meeting was proceeding to appoint a committee to fix the positions of the lamps when Mr. MATHER said he did not like committees when the matter had been arranged before the meeting. Mr. THOMPSON : I don't think that is a fair remark. The CHAIRMAN: I don't think the matter has been arranged. _ Messrs. Longton, Ball, Brooks and Knowles were appointed to form the committee. THE HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. The report of the medical officer of health showed that during the month there had been 21 birtbs and 14 deaths. The death rate was equal to 13.7 per 1,000 as against 22 the average of the past three months and 35 for the corresponding period of last year. No deaths bad occurred from diseases in the zymotic class. Ten of the deaths occurred at the County Asylum exclusive of which the death rate proper for the district was equal to 3.9 per 1,000. The measles epidemic at Padgate had continued since the last meeting and the schools had been closed for another fortnight. The epidemic had now passed away. ,

MY DEAR RELATIONS,-

I suppose your curiosity has reached its highest point in wondering who on earth is going to get Uncle Ned's three prizes for the best Christmas Day Essay. Well, after reading them through several times, I have decided to award the first prize to "L. G. F.," the second to Elsie Lewis, and the third is in Margaret Mary Press's favour. Now, how many of you guessed the correct three? I am giving you a further three contributions this week, the longest of which is very descriptive, but, un- fortunately, it is not confined to " Christmas Day," otherwise it would certainly have stood a very good chance. Next week I shall have very great pleasure in telling you who are tne favoured members for the Postcard Competition, but I hope before then to receive several more. Who'll be among the next fifty to send me a card? Last year, as most of you remember, we spent many d pleasant half-hour in solving Riddles, Enigmas, etc., and I don't think we can do much better than revivs this interesting feature, and give a couple of prizes each week. I think all my little friends will agree with me in this. I should like to start this soon after the Postcard Competition is settled, but I must not give any definite date, as it will take me some little time to find up some real easy puzzles, with the chance of your not having seen them before.

UNCLE NED.

WELCOME TO

Margaret Canham.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l should like to become a member of your "Children's Corner," and I enclose a short piece about the holidays, which I hope you will publish. I enjoyed my. Christmas holidays very much. Santa Claus 'brought me a lot of nice things, and put them in my stocking. I went to grandma's on Christmas Day, and had a nice concert. with my young uncles, dressed up in paper frocks and blouses,. and I came back on Boxing night., and I have been to two of my little friends' paTties since then. I enjoyed myself very much, but I have had a bad cold, hut now it is better, I shall be glad when Monday conies, so I can go to school again.

I remain, your loving Niece,

E. ASTLE.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
"HOW I SPENT MY HOLIDAY."

I started my holidays on the 19th of Decem- ber. At two o'clock on that day I was in the train, starting for London. We had a very pleasant journey, and when we arrived at Lon- don, one of my uncles was waiting for us. We had sent our luggage on, and so we did not have to bother about anything. When we got out of Euston Station, we got on a motor-'bus. It was a beautiful night, and there was a lovely fresh breeze blowing. As we sped along on the 'bus, we had not very much chance of seeing the sights, but still I could see a few important buildings and places. We went down the new Kingsway. ',Chere were some very fine build- ings there. When we went over Waterloo Bridge the Thames lookgd very beautiful. It seemed as if hundreds of little fairy lamps were sparkling on it. Pisaw the new cars which run along the Embankment, and I saw the famous Cleopatra's Needle. Waterloo Bridge is sup- posed to be the finest granite bridge in the country. After that/ I did not pass any place of very great importance. I quite forgot about the Tower; that, at least, is important. I only saw it in the distance. On Friday, 21st, I was again in a train on my way to Portsmouth. We went through some pretty country. The Town Hall is a very fine one. I did not go out much tliere, as it was so very cold. On Christmas Day all the trees sparkled with the snow. It was only a very slight fall of snow. It looked more like frost. The next day we went back to London. The snow was very deep there. I stayed in London until New Year's Day, and then we came home. I found a lot of Christmas cards waiting for me, and several parcels. I have had nineteen presents, and about thirty-four cards. We spent a most enjoyable holiday.

C. SAUNDERS.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l have read the "Children's Corner for a few weeks, and thought I should like to become a member. I am sending you some riddles. -Hoping to see this little letter in print, if you have room, I will now close, hoping you are well.

MARGUERITE CANHELM.

Christmas,

I remain,

GEORGE EASTWOOD.

Dear -Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a little tale, which amused me very much when I heard it, and perhaps it may amuse many others if you think it worth printing. In a public-house, a quarrel was going on be- tween a number of tall men and .a small man, the end of it being that the small man was thrown out into the road. The small man then stopped a passer-by, and said to him, "I'm going in again; you count them as I throw them out." After a long silence, the doom were heard to rattle, and a man was thrown out. "One," shouted the passer-by. "Hold on, mate, it's me come back again," said the little man. From your loving Niece,

MARJORIE E. SCOTT.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l:was so plea Fed toSe9' my piece in print.. I am sending another short story, called, "Oranges- and Lemon.' which I hope you will like. Hoping all my cousins are free from cold; and my dear Uncle and all, With love, from

W. ELLIOTT.

ORANGES AND LEMONS.

Most of the old nursery rhymes It'ava some meaning in them, but they were made so long ago that the things which everyone knew at that time have, many of them, been fcrgotten in these later days. The nursery song of "Oranges and Lemons,'' for instance, is only a tale, in verse, of what W 2.13 once Ihe "way of exacting the river toll from the fruit porters— the men who carried the boxes of oranges and lemons, or raisins and figs, from the ships to the quay. Each of these porters had to pay a fee of five oranges, or five lemons, E very New Year's Day to the Benchers of .St. Clement's Inn, and if the men had not the oranges just then, they might pay in farthings instead. You remember, it says in the rhynie, " You owe me five farthings."

With love, from

W. ELLIOTT,

HOW. I SPENT CHRISTMAS

I spent Christmas this year very quietly. I still keep up the old custom of hanging up my stockings for Santa Claus to fill, although I am twelve. Christmas-day fell on TuesdA:, this year. I think Christmas week is the jolliest and busiest time in the whole year. I had a walk as far as my brother's house op Christmas morning, so as to have an appetite for dinner. I amused my nephew and niece by taking them some toys as a Christmas box. My nephew's present was an engine on rails, my •2iece's being a set of cups and saucers. My brother gave me a present in return. It consisted of pencils, pen nibs, and pens, etc., to fill my pencil box with, which I got in my stocking. After tea my father worked the magic lantern that my brother reoeived from the well-known Father Christmas. There were twelve slides, with five pictures on each slide. We were all interested in the pictures, and never noticed the large hand of the clock creeping slowly- round to bedtime. After the Christmas holidays were over, we were kept busy taking down mistletoe, holly, and various decorations. Hoping you had as pleasant a .Christmas as I had,

MAGGIE EASTIVOOD.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am writing you this letter, asking you if you will, accept me and my .sister Dolly as your nephew and niece. I am eight years old, and Dolly is six years old. We both go to school. We used to live at Cloughfold, but we hike living here, as it is such a nice place. We have a large garden at the back of our house, and. had some lovely roses and flowers in last summer. We have some pigeons for our pets. One of them is very tame, and will eat from Dolly's hand, and stand on her head. Dadda reads "Children'a Corner" for us every week, which we enjoy very much. Hoping you win accept. us. With love, we 'remain, your loving. HARTLY and DOLIN TWEEDALE.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Bevisey-road Wesleyan Churn.

• On Sunday at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, the choir held a special Musical Festival. At the morning, service,which was conducted by the Rev. Joseph Howard, the anthem " Come ye faithful " was well interpreted. The afternoon service was presided over by the Rev. Sidney Pitt, in the unavoidable absence of Mr. Joseph Bailey who had kindly promised to occupy the chair. Mr. E. H. Pearce, principal bass,contributed in his usual fine style the two solos, "Thou art passing hence " (Sullivan) " The Lord worketh wonders" (Handel). Misses Leicester, Pitt and Evans, sang the very pretty trio, "Lift Thine eyes unto the hills" with great effect. The Male Choir gave two selections, the one entitled "Perfect in Christ "being worthy of special note. Miss Hipkiss and Misses Tynan, June and Cade also added to the afternoon's enjoyment. The Rev. Sydney Pitt was the preacher for the evening service, and his address was very appropriate to the occasion. The solo " Light of the World" was efficiently rendered by Miss Pitt and the choir also gave the anthem," The King of Love." The service was followed by a violin and organ recital. Mr. R. H. Pearson, solo violin, accom- panied by Mr. T. W. Pearce, organist, delighted the audience with several fine selections,whizh were greatly appreciated. Mr. E. H. Pearce also sang and the male choir repeated the chorus " Perfect in Christ " by special request. Much praise is due to Mr. W. Hipkiss, the choirmaster. for the successful manner in which he trained the choir. •

===== 0002601_19070309_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Christian Endeavour Convention.

The Warrington and District Union held their Spring Convention on Saturday, and the meetings were success- ful in the best sense of the ward. The afternoon com- menced with a prayer meeting led by Rev. J. S. Hughes. It was followed by a Conference presided over by Rev. H. Wall in the abgence of Rev. J. Yonge. Mrs. Towers of Chester, in speaking of " Bells," reminded us why we had been set ringine, in our different spheres of life, and earnestly entreated that we should ring true, and be ready to pus the ringing test, " A cracked bell," she said reminds no one of anything but itself." Mr. Robert Lewis of Garswood. spoke on " Loyalty to the Society," specially emphasising the value of individ- uality. Mr. R. Liddle, of Seacombe, sang " Galilee," and a Free Parliament was conducted by Rev. H. Wall, which resolved itself into a testimony meeting when earnest words were spoken by several visiting Christian Endeavour members.

After tea a most interesting School of Methods on Junior Work took place, conducted by Miss Alice Raws, of Liverpool. Much interest was shown in the various useful appliances to be used in working amongst the little ones, especially in the model of a pillar post box, in which the members of the Birthday Committee post birthday letters to the various members who happen to have a birthday near at hand. Much talking was done which will result, it is hoped, in much work. The evening meeting was presided over by Rev. J. S. Hughes, who introduced the new President (Rev. F. Vaughan). After a rousing address, in TA hid' faithful work was urged in tbe various societies, the meeting was addressed b y Rev. Bramwell Dutton, of Ashton- under-I.:me, who spoke on Vision." Earnest helpful' words recommended all present to look up, that-the feet might follow as the eye led. Time alone with God spent in communion with Him, and love as the life's motive, were means by which men become illuminated and which make possible the dawn of new life in the soul. He said "The valley, is full of graves with epitaphs written to careers that ended unfulfilled, because men look down on themselves and have no vision, and no incentive to climb." After a solo by Miss Suther- land, of Seacombe, "The Secret of His presence," Mr. MeNellen gave an address on "Progression." and the Roll Call of Societies ended a very happy Convention, which will long be remembered.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A. Anniversary.

I The fonrteenth anniversary in c onnection with the I Wycliffe P.S.I. was celebrated on Sunday the proceed- ings being attended by a crowded congregation, the Church betn, filled in every part. The Rev. John Yong° presided and in a brief address said a great deal had happened since the P.S.A. was started in 1893. Many of their brethren had left them they believed for the better land. whllst others were scattered all over England and other parts of. the world. He had no doubt that those of ten thought of the first Sunday in March 1893 and he di d not know that they or any of the brethren had ever felt the slightest regret that on that day the P.S.A. was inaugurated. They might have had regrets that they h ad not been able to do all they hoped to be able to do b ut they could say that the managers of the Society had done their level best to keep it going and to make it useful, and he hoped that in the yeaws to come they might look forward to a wider work and even greater good being accomplished. His own feelings were that they must answer their own prayer. It was , all very well to pray for success but it was little ' use praying unless they worked for success and •he would like to lay it as a Christian duty upon all the members of the Society, that they should make up their Iminds to do all they could to enlarge the number of the brotherhood and to bring into it men whom perhaps they thought the very unlikeliest. He believed the I greatest good which bad been done had been amongst men whose past had perhaps been bad and who bad been brought to the P.S.A.and come under the influence of the Word of God.and been rescued from lives of sin and evil and brought into a life of goodness, sobriety and truth. That was exactly the kind of work which the P.S.A. was meant to accomplish. If it did n9t prove itself , missionary it proved itself more or less a failure. It was not intended simply, for the edification and comfort ;of men already enlisted in the various churches but I must reach the outsiders, get them into the House of I God and engender in them a love of Sabbath observance I and sanctuary attendance and so far as it accomplished that it answered the end of its existence and gave glory !to God. (Applausel.—qr. .T. Harrison, of Liverpool. ; a well-known worker in the P.S.A. tnovement,delivered ! a vigorous address on the supreme duty in life—" Seek first the Kingdom of God and His Righteousness " urging that in their search after wealth, health or happi- ness, they should not lose sight of the injunction of the • text.—During the proceedings several enjoyable selec- tions were rendered by Messrs. Crosfleld's band, whose playing was warmly applauded.

DRESS-FA.I,:IION-HOME.7.

DECEASED WIFE'S SJsrE Bat

The "Deceased Wife's Sister " is a most ins,- pressib3e lady, and it is to be hoped. says the Gentlewoman, that :,'•7 W. Brampton Gurdon's bill, which was carried in the House of Com- mons recently by a majority of 229, will eventu- ally give her the privilege for which she has long been clamouring, viz., the right to marry the husband of her late sister. There do not seem to be very many points of argument against this bill being brought into force. It is contended that it would " weaken the marriage law," but one cannot very well see how this could possibly result. At any rate, such marriages are now legal in all other parts of the Empire. Eighteen times the house of Lords de- stroyed the bill after its affirmation by the House of Commons but in this case their power of resistance has been weakened by the passing last Session of the Colonial Marriages Bill.

A SMART SILK SHIRT.

Pictured in our illustration ,is the smartest and prettiest of up-to-date blouses. The material in *Well it is realised is a soft taffetas, in colour a rming pale blue. As you will observe, tho blouse is rather suggestive of the " shirt " in style, and it is just this faint suggestion of prim neatness which gives all its chic to this fascinat- ing model. The front of the blouse is coti- posed of a double-breasted plastron of diago- nally-striped silk, black satin stripes upon a

white ground. This plastron is cut away above the line of the bust, to display the daintiest of small chemisettes, composed of tucked white Chiffon, finished at the throat with a wee black satin bow. From the neck of the blouse turns back a small collar of .the pekine silk while cuffs of the same silk will finish the modish three- quarter shirt sleeves. The fulness of the blouse is arranged in two deep tucks on either shoulder, which are released just below the level of the arm.

RUSINESS WOMEN AS WIVES,

"I hate to give a responsible position to a woman here," says the librarian of a largo public library in New York, because every time a woman becomes valuable she throws up the position to marry." Business men, remarks the New York Mate, everywhere echo the same sentiment. And the business woman makes the best kind of a Wife, for, accustomed to reaching the office, or the schoolroom, or the factory between seven and eight in the morning, she is not the wife to finish a dream while. her husband goes off to a restaurant breakfast. She has the , getting-up habit. She is as neatly dressed to receive a caller at ten a.m. in her home as she was to take a dictation or wait on a customer. Again a little matter of habit. - When her husband comes home tired out after a clay of petty annoyances or a series of things going wrong, she does not fly into a rago be- cause he forgot the gloves she must have for the theatre that night, or weep herself into a sick headache when he votes for an evening at home instead of the play. For the woman who has sat at the typewriter till her temples throb, who has stood hours combining every tint and shade of colour to please fussy customers, who has smiled and told fairy tales to forty wriggling chi!dren when she felt as if she would fly, knows what it is to come home cross, rain-drenched, and exhausted.

The wage-earning woman may not be a chef, but the courago which staunchly faces the bread- and-butter queition does not go down before the making of a pie. Burden-bearing gives her sys- tem, neatness, a knowledge of the value of money, and, more than all, that perfect under- standing and large sympathy that makes of woman a helpmeet.

THE BILL PASSED. The House of Lords Committee, presided over by Lord Leith of Fyvie, on Thursday, passed the Bill pro- moted by the Manchester Ship Canal Company, empowering the company, for the purpose of supplying and maintaining the canal, to pump water from the estuary into the section between Latchford and Trlam locks. Agreed clauses were inserted in the interests of - Colonel Pilkington anti Mr. Beecham whose land at Warrington the Crua Comnany propose to acquire, and the Bill was ordered to be reported to the House for third reading.

===== 0002601_19070309_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
The Corporation's Position.

We understand that a clause will be inserted in the Bill preventing any greater abstraction of water than that sanctioned by the Ship Canal Act of 1885 and that clauses have been inserted preserving the rights of the public in the footpath along Arpley meadows and safeguarding the Corporation as to the height of spoil banks.

TOWN COUNCIL AND THE OPPOSITION. THE PAYMENT OF COSTS.

The Town Council had under discussion at their meeting on Tuesday the matter of the opposition to the Ship Canal Bill the immediate question by that of the payment of costs. On the 15th ult. the River Committee met to confer with the traders, manufacturers and the Rivers Preservation Societv,represeuted as follows:—Messrs.R. T. Fairclough,John Fairelough, H. Roberts. Dr. Markel, F. W. Monks, J. C. Ridgway, John White, Henry Oreenall, W. P. Rylands, Herbert Bleckly, Henry Bleckly, W. Carson,and A. Wilson, the traders solicitor, and discussed in full the several clauses of the Manchester Ship Canal (Various Powers) Bill, 1907, and -ultimately it was unanimously resolved, that this meeting of the Rivers Committee, and of manufacturers ii-nd traders is of opinion that the powers which the Manchester Ship Canal Company seeks to obtain by the Bill of 1907 are highly prejudicial to the interests of the Corporation and Traders, and requests the Corporation to offer the most strenuous opposition to the Bill, and to obtain all necessary advice and expert assistance for such purpose. The Town Clerk reported that he had filed a petition against the Manchester Ship Canal (Various Powers) Bill, 1967.

-I.i.krott proposed, and Alderman FORSTIAW seconded the apoption of the minutes. Councillor PF.MBERTOS asked if it was understood that the Corporation were not going to pay the costs of the traders. The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK said the Corporation were represented in their opposition to the Bill by separate counsel. They would, of course, pay the expenses of their own opposition, and any expenses that were incurred by the traders,who were separately represented would, he expected. be paid by the traders. There was no resolution of the Corporation to pay the traders' expenses.

Councillor PEMBERTON said the Deputy Town Clerk's reply W 35 hardly definite enough. He said "he expected." The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK: I cannot say what this Corporation will do in three months time. .1 am not a prophet. Councillor PEMBERTON: How far will anyone real. lug the resolution say we are bound by anything which the traders do? If it is distinctly understood that wa pay only our own costs I am satisfied but let there be a distinct understanding. Ido not want it to be said in twelve menthe' time that we are committed to an expenditure of from £5,000 to £lO,OOO. The MAYOR pointed out that the Corporation were

LOCAL ELECTIONS.

Warrington Board of Guardians.

MANY CONTESTS.

A surprising a wakening of interest has been evi- denced in Warrington in the triennial election of Poor Law Representatives, and subject of course to candi- dates withdrawing from the field, contests will take place in eight out of the ten wards. The whole of the members of the Board of Guardians retire and out of the twenty eight, twenty-four are seeking re-election. The four who have retired from the board are Mrs. Leah Clay, Whitecross Ward,Mr. William Hickman,St. Austin's (No. 1), Councillor T. H. Ashcroft, St. Austin's (No. 2), and Mr. George Thompson, Great Sankey. In Warrington there is a prospect of several exhilirating contests and one of the most interesting will be in the Town Hall Ward where Mr. A. J. Jollev's re-election is challenged by Mr. A. B. Marsh. Mr. Jolley has a long record of past services on the Board, but his opponent who has the support of the Grocers' and Property Owners' Associations and of several Friendly Societies, will make a big effort to capture the seat. In Latch- ford and St. Austin's (No. 2) Wards three cornered fights are promised. In Latchford Miss Broadbent is opposed by the Rev. J. F. Matthews and Mr. Edwin Plinston whilst for the seat renderel vacant by the retirement of Mr. Ashcroft. the Rev. M. Ryan, Mrs.. Osborne and Mr. William Throssell will try concltisions Mr. W. McNeil Sloane. who unsuccessfully contested Orford Ward at the last municipal election is again seeking the support of the burgesses, in opposi- tion to Mrs. Wilkinson, the retiring represent .tive. In our last issue Mrs. Simpson published a vigorous letter against the return to the Board of persons interested in the sale of intoxicating liquors and follows up her epistle by opposing Mr. James Payton, wine and beer seller, of Allen-street, wno for some years has repre- sented Bewsey Ward. In Fairfield Ward Mr. Robert Bolton also has to face opposition from the weaker sex, Mrs. Carruthers having been nominated whilst Coun- cillor Evans and Mr. James Andrew Heywood both appeal to the electors in Whitecross Ward. In the Great Sankey district Mr. T. Halton, a former representative on the Board again seeks election and is opposed by Mr. William Farness. A three cornered fight will be witnessed in Burtonwood where Mr. Robert Forshaw,, Mr. William Fairclough and Mr. James lintton are candi- dates. In Hdughton Mr. William Stanier opposes Mr. J. VW Knowles whilst in Rixton Mr. A. Brooks has,been challenged by Mr. James Robinson. Tuesday is the last day for withdrawals and the poll will take place on the 25th inst. Appended isa list of persons nominated, those marked with an asterisk being retiring members.

RETURNED UN-OPPOSED. C ITERDLF.Y.

* John Backhouse, Marsh End Farm, farmer.
